thank you, mr president, and thank you to the reporters and all those who have contributed to our work for almost a year. my report on the implementation of the european regulation on plant protection products, which the commissioner has kindly mentioned and which was adopted by a majority in this parliament last year, has shown quite clearly that there are gaps in our authorisation system which ultimately result in insufficient protection of public health and the environment. today, therefore, thanks to the work of the special committee, we have on the table a truly excellent comprehensive recommendation on how to substantially improve this european authorisation system for pesticides and restore citizens' confidence in our decision-making. i am therefore rather shocked that my colleagues in the ecr group are trying to make this joint work worthless by means of amendments in the plenary. their proposals cut across the board, aiming to better protect particularly vulnerable groups such as children, pregnant women, and seeking to block, among other things, proposals to assess developmental neurotoxicity, the long-term effects of pesticides, exposure to sub-limits of several pesticides at once or combinations of these substances. the goal is food safety and protecting health and the environment, and i absolutely don't understand why conservatives would want to deny this information to both decision makers and citizens themselves. this is ridiculous. i would like to remind you, mr president, that all european legislation is to be based on the precautionary principle of the treaties, which we must apply unconditionally whenever there is scientific uncertainty about risks to human health, animal health and environmental impact. the substance of the proposed measures is to help eliminate this scientific uncertainty, but also, of course, to improve farmers' access to safer alternatives to chemical pesticides and to increase the transparency of the whole approval system, which is in the interests of everyone. i believe that the vote on this report is one that the european public will be watching and remembering in about 130 days.
mr. chairman, i was at the beginning when the mandate for this committee was written. that is why i remember well the arguments that came out of the mouths of the right-wing factions in particular. i remember being annoyed a few times that if the mandate was supposed to be what they wanted it to be, there was no point in setting up a committee. some of the worst measures to get nothing investigated have been avoided, but the rest have not. unfortunately, the result of the work is the same. on the one hand, we have a lot of positive things, like addressing plagiarism, transparency of studies, conflicts of interest, suggestions for improving drug reviews. however, then there are questions about glyphosate or the sustainable use of pesticides that have simply been left behind. overall, the negotiations in committee did not go very well, with the constant rejection of experts proposed by the opposition being only one of the margins. i would urge you not to weaken this weak report any further in the plenary vote. if that happens, the result of a year's work will be nothing more than the start of a campaign for some of us.
i was not one of the advocates for the establishment of the pest special committee. in general, i believe that issues should be dealt with in standing committees rather than ad hoc committees. special committees always tend to politicize and ideologise the issue more. but in the case of the withdrawal from the pest committee, i can congratulate our group in the european people's party for ensuring that this text is balanced and realistic. it calls for greater independence and expertise in pesticide authorisation procedures, which is certainly appropriate. it's good to have the glyphosate review. nor do i object to the claim that the use of plant protection products for the purpose of drying crops before harvesting is inappropriate from the point of view of human health.
madam president, i would like to remind you that the last report on mainstreaming gender equality in the work of the european parliament was discussed here in 2016 and included a call for equal representation in the committee on women's rights and gender equality. but almost three years have passed, and little has changed in that regard. we are nowhere near equal representation in this committee, which should be symbolic for the european parliament. i think if we don't achieve that goal, it's very ridiculous, let me say, sadly hypocritical, that we want to educate others about the need for balanced representation of women and men. we cannot achieve this on our own in a committee that is supposed to be concerned with this very objective. i'll remind you of the number 7 men out of 33 regular members, 10 men out of 29 alternates. that's not really equal representation. i consider the establishment of this committee and the consideration of such reports, unfortunately, only as a certain alibi. we have a strategy, we have a report, we have a resolution, but basically nothing substantial is going to change. i think our goal really has to be equality between women and men. protecting women in their natural role, and that's the mother role. let's support women to develop their professional careers, let's support women in their dignified lives, in their role as mothers, so that they can properly take care of their families.
madam president, i would like to say that nuclear energy is a low-carbon alternative to fossil fuels for most of the member states of the european union and is also a key component for energy generation in their energy mix. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the nuclear safety and security policy and will continue to monitor the implementation of the nuclear safety and security policy. we are talking about a research project that is undoubtedly delayed, has its problems, probably also comes late, these capital expenditure increases must be paid precisely in view of the fact that this research, this fusion reactor part simply can be a very significant step forward and can also give us some incentive to meet the paris climate targets, so i take this report with the knowledge that it is a serious delay, but in any case it represents a certain possible step forward for us.
madam president, i would also like to point out this unusual situation, that in the czech republic, for example, the public administration system has been undergoing reform since its inception. this is a very slow and complex process and it is important to use the eu budget for public administration reform. it is clear that, for example, cohesion policy resources should not replace the member state where the national source of funding should be used, that is to say, it should no longer be regarded as a complementary system, and of course this union budget should again be primarily result-oriented. the positive impact of the use of this budget then also has an impact on the public administration of the member states and this effect is then multiplied. i would also point out that we should not only focus on the issue of funding public reporting, but also on the exchange of information. it's an agenda that actually uses learning to share information with each other. these are things i took the liberty of observing without presenting the report to the reporter.
i would like to emphasize, madam president, the report by mr grzyb, because i think it is really very good and we should remember that we stand for the principle of equality. that means equality for both believing and unbelieving groups. what mr jurek said then, although he was right, i also regret very much when i see a certain incoherence in european policy, in the sense that we deal with and provide development aid, often to countries which persecute christians. that's not a good example. on the other hand, the path chosen by the european union, that it wants to appoint a special envoy for the issue of religious freedom, i think is very correct and i want to support it, because religion is sometimes pushed to two extremes today. either religious groups are persecuted or religion is used as a political instrument. we should push our position, which means equal rights, but also the guarantee of religious freedom, which is given by the european charter, to which we all certainly subscribe.
madam president, i believe that the establishment of the function of special envoy for the promotion of freedom of religion or belief outside the european union is a very important and good decision. he is also happy that the first to hold this position since 2016 is the slovak diplomat and christian democrat ján figeľ. it was he who, together with czech diplomats in sudan, negotiated the release of czech humanitarian worker petr jašek. he was sentenced to 20 years in prison for documenting the persecution of christians in sudan. his release followed, and i believe it was also the initiative of former commissioner figeel, but also of the european parliament and the commission, together with czech diplomacy, which helped to free petr jasek. there are still millions of convicted and persecuted people and it is important not to relax in their protection.
madam president, i understand the intention of colleagues who are trying to establish equality between women and men, they want to reduce the gender pay gap, but colleagues, this is not the right path you have chosen. we cannot violate subsidiarity in the interests of this objective. taxation, the tax system, is a matter for the member states. and i will always be in favour of the possibility of using, for example, family taxation, the right of families to benefit from tax reductions, because it is the best instrument to help to consolidate families, to take advantage of the fact that women are thereby favoured, if the member state has this tax advantage. this message is going in exactly the opposite direction. i am very sorry, i respect my colleague urthasu, for example, who is one of the few brave men who is a member of the committee on women's rights and gender equality, but this is not a good solution.
madam president, i do not wish to minimize the subject, but i would certainly like to say that there are a number of very instructive examples and positive points in the report. in general, i would like to emphasize that inequality can be observed in all societies in terms of access to services, in the increasing income gap between men and women. i'd like to throw some ash on my head here, because the czech republic has a pretty significant gender pay gap. a man and a woman are equal partners to me, of course. however, in the case of tax policy, it is clear that their revenues are simply shared, and this is what the report also points out. i think we should specify this role for the member states in particular, because they are the ones who have an irreplaceable role in this case, and they should create a social system, a tax system that is equivalent. that means starting from the equal principle. i do not want to question the motivation of my colleagues for submitting this report, but at the same time i think that subsidiarity should be given a preferential place in this respect.
madam president, commissioner, i am of course aware that automated mobility has the potential for many sectors, as it offers new business opportunities for start-ups, of course smes, which are at the heart of our interest, and also for industry and businesses as a whole, especially in terms of creating new services in the field of mobility, and also has the potential to create jobs, which, however, also has the potential to be reduced. we deal with a whole range of legal aspects, security and, for example, reliable route data. the issue of insurance, special insurance, we're already solving the problem of congestion on existing infrastructure, and this congestion problem is not going to be solved by autonomous driving. the problem of cars in cities is here to stay. i certainly support this particular operation in public transport, but as the rapporteur has said, we still have a lot of work to do in the area of legislation, which should not be underestimated.
madam president, exactly 50 years ago, a student named jan palach set himself on fire in prague's wenceslas square. it was january 16, 1969. three days later, he died of burns. why would you do that? it was to remind us that he wanted to provoke the apathy of both czechs and slovaks against the occupation, against censorship, against the loss of the ideal of the prague spring. he wanted to stir up the conscience of the nation to resist. the strength of his protest struck a chord with the conscience and soul of both czechs and slovaks, who then returned to his legacy with pride 30 years ago during the so-called palach week, when they protested against the then communist dictatorship. jan palach sacrificed his life, but it was not in vain, because we live today in freedom, in the community of european nations. but his sacrifice is relevant today because the lies and intimidation continue to be spread. that is why we must remember his sacrifice today.
on behalf of the ecr group. – mr president, generally speaking, the austrian presidency was well-crafted, well-organised and efficient – that’s because austria is of course a well—functioning country – so you covered a lot of issues. let me do just a little cherry picking to pick up just a few of them. i will start of course with migration and security. i have to tell you that it was really refreshing to see a slightly different point of view from the presidency and the chancellery than we have heard many times before. you indicated very clearly what many other politicians were hesitant to say, namely that europe is neither ready nor willing to accept continuous waves of migrants, as it would have an impact on both our security and the internal coherence of our societies. you also came forward with proposals and solutions. for instance, we’ve seen improvements of the schengen borders code or the entry—exit system, which will both help to manage migration and help us to tackle illegal migration. i can assure you your message was heard and appreciated, particularly in central and eastern europe and in the v4 countries. again it was proven how important it is to find a consensus between the member states instead of arbitrary solutions, like the very unfortunate relocation scheme pursued by the commission, which unfortunately caused one of the most serious clashes yet between east and west and between new and old eu member states. i hope that is definitely off the table. you also made it one of your priorities to ensure the prosperity and competitiveness of the european economy, especially through digitalisation and innovation, and you were right. while we have a well—established and functioning start-up culture, the biggest players often come from outside the european union. we need to do more for start-ups and for small and medium-sized enterprises. in this respect, we need to remove trade barriers. i would also like to congratulate you on the swift ratification of the free trade agreement with japan, which happened during your presidency. this creates the world’s largest free trade area, covering nearly 30% of global gdp. i hope that other similar treaties still in the pipeline – most notably with singapore and vietnam – will follow soon. last but not least, the multiannual financial framework negotiations continued under your presidency. they are not over yet, and that is good news, because this parliament and this commission are finishing their mandate, and therefore they should not decide on behalf of the member states or commissioners of the next term. external relations were also a big burden for you. i will not go further into that, but i will end by stating that the austrian presidency was pragmatic, non-ideological and not a presidency of big slogans, but rather of achievable results. this is good news. it was good to see common sense once again prevailing in most of your endeavours, because common sense is exactly what my own group is trying to pursue in this parliament.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, chancellor, i’m not sure whether you could bear even more congratulations, because we heard a lot today. so i will spare you by only saying that i think that that was an efficient presidency and austria did deliver as a presidency on its intentions. but i rather look at always every six-months period of time, not as a presidency issue but how did the european union really do, and how have we advanced, regardless of the presidency? and i think that this needs to be said. this is still a european union of 28 – maybe tomorrow 27 – member states where the presidency has a key role to play, but obviously it cannot perform miracles, but it can help a great deal. i think that the balance sheet, as i’ve said, is pretty good. but if you speak of the number of trilogues, i agree with you: i think this is again a sign of an efficient austrian presidency. but let’s look into the legislation that we have enacted: is it really always the right legislation which will really deliver on the three priorities that you had or on the priorities that we will have in the future? i’ll give you just one example. together with the rapporteur angelika niebler, we dealt with cyber security. i think the result is not bad at all. in fact, it’s a good result, and also i must say, even despite some clashes that we had with your ambassador (but that is a normal part of the trilogue), i need to say that it still reflects the lack of change of mind-set in the member states. we still look at cyber security as an issue where we tack between those who would like to prevail in terms of competences and the rest, and we are as strong or as weak as the weakest part of the chain of the european union. so that is just one example where i would like to say the numbers matter and you have been efficient, but it also matters what kind of legislation we are able to enact, how the member states are ready and, of course, how we manage to strike compromises with real ambition. on competitiveness, on digital: yes, again i think that this is significant progress, but still at the same time, if i had the brain (and i don’t) and i invented an application, would i commercialise it in the european union or would i go to silicon valley? and the answer is clear: i still would go to silicon valley. so there is a lot to do in terms of the european union’s ecosystem. let me just focus on two or three issues where i think we could have done better, for sure, and maybe that history will show also the presidency. one is on internal security. i do agree with you and i think it was a balanced approach, but still let’s admit that we still have a block in the council on a number of proposals which are still pending. and we are not facing today a crisis, we are facing a problem, and the moment the crisis would come, we could have significant difficulties on the budget. and i think that again, there is enormous progress – you have ticked all the boxes; the question is: how well were these boxes filled? and i must say i don’t agree with mr zahradil, who says that that should be left to the next parliament. no, i think we have been elected or appointed for the whole term. but i think that he would have to explain, whether it’s on cohesion terms or to farmers, that, when you are really lagging behind, it has an impact on your businesses. but what i would like to say on the budget is that we should still proceed, and that’s maybe one critical remark also to austria. i think that this is not realistic. i’m definitely against any waste of money, but i think that we need to address the fact that we have new challenges and we just can’t keep, without a serious revision of some of the policies, the budget, and eventually the sum will increase. but anyway, at the very end, i think with those remarks i think that you have done a very good job, but we as the european union are not yet doing the job that our citizens really expect us to do, and we do fail on some issues. in writing. i too would like to wish romania a successful presidency. one of the most important legislative proposals we have on the table in the committee on the internal market and consumer protection is the package on the modernisation of consumer law. it also includes a proposal that, after years of proof and explanation, will finally put an end to the so-called dual quality of products. adapting the composition of branded products to where they are sold is not in line with the basic principles of the internal market. double quality must be an unfair commercial practice and duly penalized. the addition of the unfair commercial practices directive is a unique opportunity. let's not be fooled by arguments about preferences the world has never seen. romania has its own negative experience according to the tests carried out with double quality. it should therefore be more sensitive to the legitimate concerns of consumers than the previous austrian presidency and use this opportunity to strengthen citizens' confidence in a fair internal market.
mr president, i support the proposal for a revision of the regulation on the european globalisation adjustment fund and particularly welcome the amendment concerning both the reduction of staff and the extension of the period for assessing whether the conditions have been met. it is precisely from the perspective of smaller countries such as the czech republic that this change is for the better. in the future, this should make the application procedure easier to use and simplify for member states, and shorten the time limit for the commission to assess the application. i also support parliament's position that the fund should be able to finance incentives for employers to offer flexible working hours. this is important for parents, and these incentives make a lot of sense in terms of getting back to work and reconciling work and life.
mr president, i would of course like to express my support for this instrument and for the instruments of european policy, because it really helps disadvantaged groups of citizens. it also helps the regions that suffer the consequences of globalisation and the sectors of our economy that are affected. i have to say that i felt from the beginning that this globalisation fund was a great idea, and i think that if our european union policy was to address these very factors that affect the lives of people who lose their jobs, we could eliminate the brexit dispute. i was somewhat inspired to make this speech by the speech of my colleague mr simon, and i want to support him very much, i think that focusing on these factors that affect ordinary people is in the central interest of the european union and we should further deepen this instrument and work with it as a quality instrument to improve the quality of life of citizens.
mr president, i believe that this new regulation on the european social fund will contribute to a more efficient use of resources. there should be no overlap of funds and projects could combine several objectives. the new rules should facilitate implementation and reduce administrative burden. i also welcome the fact that funding is more closely linked to the european semester process. i believe that this should be the case for allocating resources to key priorities where member states are performing poorly. socio-economic disparities between member states should also be bridged more rapidly. i also appreciate the greater flexibility in investing resources, leaving a lot of scope for tailoring funding to the national context. in the czech republic, for example, we will be able to continue to support children's groups, micro-nursery schools and educational programmes.
mr. chairman, allow me to thank the rapporteur. the sad truth is that many social challenges persist in european society. more than 100 million citizens are at risk of poverty or social exclusion. in the czech republic alone, 863,000 people are trapped in a debt trap that has ended in execution. of course, this poses a huge problem, and one that is related to this is that people who are in debt very often do not believe in justice. these problems not only threaten the well-being of the citizens directly affected, but also put economic and social pressure on european society as a whole. and so i am very pleased that the european social fund plus will support investments in people and systems in policy areas such as employment, public services, health, education and social inclusion. the european union must never forget that the best investment is in people and in improving their quality of life. our citizens deserve the best conditions to develop freely in a peaceful society.

the next item is the topical debate (rule 153a) on comprehensive european education, research and remembrance of the totalitarian past (2019/2500(rsp)).
mr president, teaching history, teaching totalitarianism is necessary and not easy. that's why i want to remind you of the czech project memory of the nation, which is being carried out by the post bellum organization. this organization collects testimonies and distributes them to students in schools and to the community. the testimonies of the memorialists, this is a very effective method that we should be encouraging, because we know that the teachers have a key role to play, and we need to provide them with as much support and information as possible. the jan amos prize for teachers of european union studies is one such example. this pilot project was supported by the european parliament and should be implemented this year. it's our contribution and support for teachers who are supposed to encourage and inform students. and also the house of european history, which is in the hands of the european parliament. we have one specific contribution to make. on march 20th, a film about the student jan palach, who was mentioned earlier, will be shown here. today marks the 50th anniversary of the burning of his human torch.
member of the commission. mr president, i cannot resist, i must speak my language, because i also want to commemorate the sacrifice of jan palach, whose anniversary we are commemorating today. fifty years since the self-immolation of the student of the faculty of philosophy jan palach. as i listened to the debate, i realized how very strongly and how symbolically this information sounds here today in the context of totalitarianism, censorship, and i cannot help but remember the moment when my mother, when i was about 15 years old, i think it was a whisper, told me who jan palach was and what he did in 1969. i remember very clearly what she said to me: "don't say that name anywhere, but remember that name". and then came january 1989 and that name was chanted by us czechs in wenceslas square and i realized that the sacrifice that jan palach made, the self-immolation, the torturous and agonizing death that he chose, that the sacrifice was not in vain and that he had done his important work in czech society. and it should continue to do so not only in czech society, but in all of europe. i think that today, we should be aware of these connections, aware that totalitarianism brings censorship, brings backsliding, repression, emigration and moral distortion. these are all the fruits that we may be reaping today and that we must do everything against. as far as education is concerned, i think that nobody, not even the european union, which i represent, wants the education system of today to teach children the only truths that are allowed. it's the sign of totality. we want schools to give objective information, not to avoid the subject of modern history, and most importantly to teach the young generation to work with information, to lead them to critical thinking. because to think that only our education system, our schools, will ensure that we know our modern history, that's a very naive notion. we're in the internet age now, an age where, as many of you have discovered, we're being bombarded by waves of misinformation. that's why it's so important not just to give young people information, but to teach them to work with information, to teach them critical thinking and to let them work with information that they get from different sources. i think this is not just a task for the european union, but above all, as it has been said here, for the member states, on which i personally tend to really, really rely, to not allow totalitarianism to control not only education, but also our societies.
i must say that i am personally surprised and sorry that the colleagues who have expressed their concern about the european union's propaganda in the curricula here have not had enough time to hear the commissioner's reaction.

the next item is the debate on the report by josep-maria terricabras, on behalf of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs, on the situation of fundamental rights in the european union in 2017 (2018/2103(ini)) (a8—0466/2018).
mr president, i do not know why, when we are talking about such an important issue as human rights, the representatives of the council, that is to say the romanian presidency, were not invited to discuss this issue. can you please explain this to me, or are basic human rights not important to the romanian presidency?
mr president, i particularly appreciate the first part of the report, which emphasises the rule of law. the european union is nothing but a law, a legal code. it has no police, customs or army of its own. therefore, insisting on respect for the law and values such as the rule of law is an existential matter for the union. unfortunately, this report completely ignores the manifestations of militant secularism in europe. so intense are the manifestations of intolerance and discrimination against christians in europe that a research institute has been set up in austria to study the phenomenon. but it is illogical, our system of fundamental rights is based only on respect for the individual, which has christian roots. without these roots, the whole system is in danger of degenerating. answer the question by raising the blue card. i was talking about something else, i was talking about the discrimination against christians in europe. of course, any discrimination that is both illegal and harmful to some people in the european union is also not to my liking. answer the question by raising the blue card. i respect everyone's beliefs, but i would point out that respect for the individual, which is the basis of human rights, if it is cut off from christian roots and not interpreted in their spirit, it inevitably leads to degeneration, and it is already happening.
mr president, i naturally agree with the emphasis on the rule of law and respect for the separation of powers and the independence of the judiciary in the member states. it's a fact that some countries in the european union have a big problem with that right now. i also agree with the report that article 7 should be invoked only when all other means have failed, that is, only as a last resort. i also have a number of substantial reservations about this report. first of all, i do not agree with the unilateral assessment of the activities of non-profit organisations in the mediterranean. helping people is good, but this activity must not serve as a motivating factor for smugglers and illegal migration. we must remember that the most vulnerable are women and children. it's not always easy, for example, in the greek islands, and we should especially volunteer these days to help greece in this difficult winter. i strongly disagree with the report's recommendation to ratify the istanbul convention on sexual and reproductive health and sexual education. it's a purely national matter. the european union should protect the right to life, not promote abortion.
for the group gue/ngl madam president, every year thousands of small and mediumsized enterprises across the european union go bankrupt while waiting to pay their bills. late payments in europe cause many insolvencies, leading to social problems and job losses. the 2011 directive on the prevention of late payment in commercial transactions was intended to improve the situation for companies. i thank the rapporteur for mapping out and showing in detail the situation surrounding the implementation of this directive. it appears that a number of problems and obstacles still exist, such as a lack of awareness of the existence of this legal instrument. furthermore, i would point out that only a small percentage of smes have seen a positive impact on late payments from debtors as a result of this directive. ladies and gentlemen, we have a lot to improve on. i disagree with mr sulík that we should not get involved in this matter, because there is no worse enemy for smes than the treasury and its apparatus, which always plays the power play. i therefore fully support the rapporteur and her proposals for a series of measures that could lead to better enforcement of the late payments directive. the aim must be to create a level playing field for all companies. as this is, madam president, - please - my last speech in this parliament, i would like to thank everyone with whom i have had the opportunity to work over the last 17 years, from the bureau to the interpreters.

the next item is the debate on the commission statement on the state of the us—eu trade negotiations (2019/2530(rsp)).
member of the commission. – mr president, the commission is always at the disposal of parliament even if the timing of this debate has been a challenge. with the adoption of the joint statement from last july by president juncker and president trump, we entered into a new stage in our relationship with the us, our first investment and trade partner, and we identified a focused and positive trade agenda. since then, i have met with my american counterparts many times in an effort to implement the joint programme that we identified last summer. we have identified future avenues on voluntary cooperation with the us and a number of regulatory sectors of major economic importance, such as pharmaceuticals, medical devices and cybersecurity, and the progress achieved so far has been reflected in a public report that we released this morning and is available for all of you to read. we will continue to engage with the us in order to respond to the global challenges, notably the wto reform and the need to update rules to respond to the challenges posed by state capitalism. trends: trade in liquefied natural gas and soya beans are developing in line with expectations expressed in the july joint statement and have contributed to eu energy security and provided our farmers with protein at competitive prices. two weeks ago, the commission adopted two recommendations for opening negotiations, one on industrial tariffs and one on conformity assessment. the one on industrial tariffs is quite self—explanatory and it does not include agriculture, as agreed in july. the proposed mandate on conformity assessment maybe deserves a little explanation. both the eu and the us have in their respective practices of conformity assessment the purpose to ensure that manufacturers can only place a product on the market when it meets all the applicable requirements to ensure that unsafe or otherwise non—compliant products do not find their way to unsuspecting consumers. in some cases this requires mandatory third-party certification. differences in approaches to conformity assessment can result in significant additional costs without improving the safety of the products. so our aim with this recommendation for negotiations on conformity assessment is to reduce the unnecessary costs while making sure that we fully maintain our respective levels of protection. the two recommendations thereby fulfilled the commitment that we took in july. they recognise that progress with the trump administration is at this stage only possible with individual projects agreed on both sides. so i believe that the various strands of work we have initiated together through this process since july will add to a significant agenda with benefits from both sides. we can make every effort to project a positive trade agenda with the us on its own merits and also it is important not to fall back into a destructive cycle of trade restrictive measures and counter actions. at the same time, we must be very clear that any thought of going beyond the joint statement and negotiating a comprehensive and all encompassing trade agreement with the us is neither feasible nor advisable at this time. the us is also not ready to negotiate on its sensitivities, such as public procurement by american maritime services, or geographical indications. in addition, one of the important lessons that we have learnt during the ttip negotiation is that the regulatory agenda with the us is best developed in full transparency, but outside the context of fta negotiations. furthermore, there are differences in some important trade-related rule areas where opposition will be very difficult to bridge. so our efforts are more likely to bear fruit if we seek to cooperate with the us in reforming certain multilateral trade rules that are ripe for an update. we are of course remaining vigilant and we will be ready to respond firmly in case the us would no longer respect commitments given in july and would adopt further restrictive measures vis—à—vis the eu, but this scenario would not be of our choosing. i’m looking forward to the debate.
once again, for those who came later, i am afraid that for time reasons there will be no catch—the—eye either in this debate, or in any other to follow.
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, yes, we do have differences with the us administration in several areas – also in the trade area. what we are doing and what was the purpose of the meeting in july was that, despite those differences, we tried to find a common agenda, a positive platform in the trade area and to avoid further escalation, which would be bad for us, for them and for the world. this was agreed in july and what we agreed upon was published immediately. what we do now is that we are respecting that agenda, and it is also following what the european council unanimously concluded in their sofia meeting in june. there was unanimity on proposing this to the us. we have been talking with the us during this six months in full transparency. i have many times in the european parliament committee on international trade (inta) to report on this, and as i said, the full report on what has been discussed in all areas here has been online since this morning – you can read it. what we are asking the council for is two mandates: one on conformity assessment and one on industrial goods. it is industrial goods, all industrial goods including the car sector and fisheries, that are included here. it is common in the wto context that you include fisheries and industrial goods together, and it makes for a wto-compliant agreement. this is all and there is nothing else; there’s nothing hidden. this is what we propose. nothing more, nothing less. this is not a free trade negotiation that we are proposing. a full fta would, of course, include everything that you have mentioned – difficult issues for the us on public procurement, on geographical indications, on maritime services – difficult issues for us on agriculture, and a few other things. these things are not included. and furthermore, we would, of course, have urged our partner to be a signatory to the paris agreement. but this is not a free trade agreement. it’s very limited, albeit beneficial for us and for them. we’re talking about billions of euros from both sides if we were to eliminate tariffs on this. that is why the mandate is short, because this is what it is: nothing else. it is also clear that this agreement cannot be concluded unless there is a solution to the steel and aluminium issue. the tariffs have to be gone, as was said here, and it would not go ahead if the us imposed other tariffs, as is being discussed right now. so this is also clear and it is also reflected in the mandate. that was not funny, that is very serious. so this is something that would be good for mutual trade relations for our business and our consumers, and i can promise you that i will continue to keep you fully informed on where we are going on this. but what you see is exactly what there is. there is no hidden agenda here.

the next item is the report by ramón jáuregui atondo, on behalf of the committee on constitutional affairs, on the state of the debate on the future of europe (2018/2094(ini)) (a8-0427/2018).
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, it is very fitting that in recent years the european parliament has become a forum for discussion on the future of the european union, with the speakers being the prime ministers and representatives of the member states. i am very sorry that the czech prime minister, andrej babiš, refused to attend the january plenary, and that he did not actually use the invitation to share his vision for the future of the european union. the report we are discussing and voting on says that the way forward is differentiated integration and political union. i think it is true that some challenges, in particular the challenge of defence and the external and internal security of citizens, require a strengthening of the common competences. on the other hand, i call for greater respect for subsidiarity and for stopping the flight from integration at any cost. let's learn from brexit.
member of the commission. – mr president, let me start by thanking the rapporteur for this timely and highly relevant report. we have to be clear that when we discuss the future of europe we discuss whether and how we want the european union to reaffirm its future in the world. if we want our union to stay an actor in global politics in the 21st century, to maintain our economic strength, to defend our interests, and to shape foreign policy by projecting our european values of democracy, freedom, openness, solidarity, the rule of law and fundamental rights, then we have to do all what is necessary so that europe has the internal legitimacy, capacity and means to do that. at his state of the union address in this house in september last year, president juncker presented to you his priorities and outlined his vision for how the european union could devolve by 2025. on the same occasion, he presented a roadmap for a more united, stronger and more democratic union. in particular, he called for confirming the process of the so-called spitzenkandidaten at european level, completing the governance of the euro area, and for a modern and values-based european budget for the coming years. in the longer run, the commission’s take on the future of europe is that we do not exclude treaty change. today, however, we must not discuss treaty change but focus on concrete policies and on the immediate concerns of our citizens. this is what we are doing. the commission has by now fully delivered on all the proposals needed to address its priorities. together with you, the members of the european parliament, and the 28 member states, we have adopted over the last four years, around 250 ambitious legal acts in the areas of investments in the real economy, the digital union, energy union, capital markets union, internal and cybersecurity, border protection, defence, economic and monetary union, trade, research and innovation, climate change, social solidarity, youth and employment. we are thus delivering concrete results that are to the benefit of all european citizens. as we meet today, our three institutions are working night and day to conclude important pending files, such as the european border and coast guard, the prevention of terrorist content online, copyright and a series of contingency measures to mitigate the negative consequences of brexit before the next elections. it is in all our interests to give it one last push and our joint efforts are already bearing fruit. the european union is in the sixth consecutive year of economic growth. 239 million people in the european union are currently in jobs. this is the highest number ever. with this, the european union is on course to meet its europe 2020 target of a 75% employment rate by 2020. youth unemployment has fallen, down from 25% in 2013 to 15% overall, but remains far too high in some of our member states. the eu is defending a rule and values-based multilateral order as just confirmed by the partnership agreements with japan, which has just entered into force and which will generate new opportunities and growth both for europeans and our partners. these results should inspire us to not let up because the task of building a more secure, sustainable and more prosperous and fairer europe is far from over. steadying a confident and collective european course is the best response we can give to rising forces of illiberalism, nationalism and xenophobia and to fears of rising trade conflicts. on may 9 this year, just a few days before the elections to the european parliament, the leaders of the european union will meet at a summit in sibiu, romania, to reaffirm to european citizens and to the world, their commitment to a united, strong and democratic future of europe at 27. let us use this and any other opportunity to send strong and positive messages to our citizens. let us not allow the doomsayers to weaken or paralyze our union. let us convince our citizens, so that at the next elections they will want to vote for a positive agenda, for stability, freedom and prosperity, for a union that is united, strong and democratic.
the debate is closed. the vote will take place on wednesday, 13 february 2019. written statements (rule 162)
the next item is a short presentation of the report by ashley fox, on behalf of the committee on industry, research and energy, on a comprehensive european industrial policy on artificial intelligence and robotics (2018/2088(ini)) (a8—0019/2019).
mr president, mr rapporteur, mr commissioner, europe has undeniable potential in the development of artificial intelligence. however, it also raises a number of issues related to impacts that have not yet been addressed by national or eu legislation. some member states are therefore trying to adopt their own legislation to regulate autonomous robots. i think it is important that the european union responds together at eu level by harmonising the rules. the member states should work together on ai, because we face the same challenges, the same risks and together we face competition, especially from china and america. but it is not just about competitiveness, it is also about the safety of the citizens of the european union, because ai brings progress but also brings great security challenges, which is why i welcome the coordination plan for ai that the commission presented in december. i think we need to act fast.
member of the commission. – mr president, i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr fox, and the shadow rapporteurs, as well as the associated committees, for the very good report on a comprehensive european industrial policy on artificial intelligence and robotics. ai and robotics have great benefits to offer to improve quality of life. for example, precision farming promises to drastically reduce the need for pesticides and ai can also help to greatly improve cancer treatments and save lives thanks to early diagnostics. we welcome in particular that the report addresses all the key aspects, namely, that it is essential to ensure the eu's competitiveness by boosting its research and industrial capacity in ai and to promote the uptake of ai across the economy. that it is necessary to prepare for the socio-economic changes brought about by ai, in particular regarding skills, jobs and social protection. and that we have to ensure an appropriate ethical and legal framework based on the union's values and in line with the charter of fundamental rights of the eu. the commission is very pleased that parliament shares their vision on the development and deployment of human-centric ai and acknowledges the importance of achieving ethical and trustworthy artificial intelligence – the trademark for ai made in europe. concerted eu action is also essential to avoid the fragmentation of the overall legal and ethical framework, and it is important that all stakeholders are represented in the process. i am glad to know that we share the understanding that without sufficient funding, europe will not be able to regain leadership in ai. increased funding will be of crucial importance to the digital europe programme and horizon europe, accompanied by the development of a public-private partnership in ai. we should make every effort to realise the promise potentially illustrated in the report for important sectors such as healthcare, energy, transport, agri- food, cyber-security and in the public sector, increasing productivity and competitiveness as well as quality of life at work and at home. the commission appointed a high-level expert group on ai to draft ethics guidelines covering issues such as fairness, safety, transparency, democracy, personal data protection, dignity, consumer protection and non-discrimination. the group should finalise its work in the course of march, and the guidelines are expected to be published in early april. it is encouraging to see the support of parliament to build on this work for an inclusive and ethical development in the use of ai. parliament welcomes the creation of the expert group on liability and new technologies. while you regret that no legislative proposal was put forward during this legislature, you will understand that the substance is very complex. that is why the commission will put forward a report in early summer outlining the broader implications for potential gaps in, and orientations for, the liability and safety frameworks for ai. we have to build on our own strengths and support the european entrepreneurial spirit. we must be fully engaged and ensure broader and easier access to services for citizens and industry and address socio-economic and legal issues, building on european values. a united and value-based approach to ai will make the european union a leader in ai and help promote ethical principles worldwide. and this concerted effort is necessary to ensure that european citizens and the economy can reap the benefits such technology has to offer. in conclusion, the european parliament is uniquely placed in this mission, in particular in informing about the benefits ai can bring while carefully addressing the risks. the commission is delighted that it can count on the support of parliament to actively contribute to developing and implementing the comprehensive european industrial policy on ai and robotics. ai can improve quality of lives, greatly contribute to our economic growth and our environmental sustainability. let us not miss these opportunities.
the debate is closed. the vote will take place on tuesday, 12 february 2019. written statements (rule 162)
the next item is the one-minute speeches on matters of political importance (rule 163). i will underline ‘one minute’ and we will respect that time slot.
mr president, i would like to draw attention again to the situation which unfortunately is currently developing in the uk, namely the practices which are emerging in particular in relation to smugglers who smuggle people through europe to the uk. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament and the council should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work. i want to draw attention to the case of george sagan, who was sentenced to two years in a french prison, despite the fact that the police had in no way proved that he was the one who knowingly smuggled people into the united kingdom. i would like to call on the commission to really systematically look at the cases of truck drivers, because this situation is not sustainable in the long term.
mr president, last week i saw firsthand that hundreds of refugee children are living on the streets of the european union without protection. we know from the available data that there are currently 3,792 unaccompanied children in greece, of which 7% are under 14 years old. most of them have no parents, died in the war or are missing. in greece last week, i met a six-year-old girl, zafira, from afghanistan, whose parents had both died on the journey. her story touched me, but i was glad that this little girl was the lucky one, because she is placed in a children's home in athens, where she is cared for. but they are one of the lucky exceptions, because a thousand other children remain on the streets in greece, and a thousand more are in various camps and other institutions, waiting to be reunited with relatives or to have better conditions. in this context, i repeatedly appeal to governments, including the czech government, to take care of these children.
that concludes the item.
for the s&d group. mr president, the rapporteur, mrs jytte guteland, has produced an excellent report, it is clear that parliament and the commission are doing their job, which unfortunately cannot be said of the member states. the principles of integrated plant protection have been in place since 2014, but until member states start playing their part, we will only be telling ourselves stories about what they mean and why we need to follow them. we do not want to take away the tools of plant protection from farmers, but to offer them tools that do not harm their health, our health, or the environment. the use of chemical pesticides, like the use of antibiotics, should be a last resort and not the basis of our european agriculture. in this context, i am a little sorry that the commission has not heeded parliament's call for the approval of biological and low-risk pesticides to be accelerated, which is probably not going to happen until next term. in europe today we talk a lot about security, but we put a time bomb in our country every day, a time bomb that is ticking around us and will explode sooner or later. no walls or borders can protect us from it. let's finally admit that the current way of running the economy is not sustainable in the long run. if we actively change it ourselves, it will be a controlled process that does not harm farmers, consumers or the landscape. if that happens as a result of the collapse of the ecosystem, we all suffer.
i am speaking, madam president, to explain my approach, which is clearly in favour of protecting the health of the public, but we must also think of the producers of agricultural products, for whom the use of pesticides is simply a necessity for the time being. despite the pressure to reduce the use of pesticides and to ban certain pesticides containing carcinogens, it makes sense, as demonstrated by a report from the european food safety authority, which says that 96% of samples tested complied with the permitted limits and even half of the samples did not contain any quantifiable residues. i therefore believe that it is clear that this pressure is justified and that it is justified to limit the use of the most dangerous pesticides, but that it is necessary to call for restrictions on the import of foodstuffs treated with pesticides and for a ban on the import of pesticides.
for the group gue/ngl madam president, commissioner, allow me also to thank my colleague mr belet for what i consider to be an excellent report. for certain patient groups, such as those with rare diseases, cross-border healthcare is simply the most appropriate and accessible care. i believe that the importance of sharing knowledge and resources needs to be constantly emphasised, particularly in the context of rare disease treatment. today, these problems can no longer be solved simply at national level alone, but require the involvement of the material and intellectual resources of the whole eu. more efforts are also needed in the areas of mutual recognition of prescriptions from other member states, reducing red tape and all artificial barriers to reimbursement of cross-border care, increasing the use and use of the european insurance card and, last but not least, increasing the low level of patient awareness of the possibility of receiving such care. what i also welcome in the report is the call for greater harmonisation of vaccination obligations across the eu. i believe that this is an extremely important step at a time when certain regions are increasingly ravaged by epidemics of diseases that we have wrongly thought to be eradicated. and last but not least, we also have to address the negative externalities of cross-border care. i am referring again to the so-called parallel re-export of medicines. for several years now, we have been calling on the commission and the member states to prohibit this practice, which is harming the health systems of less developed member states. and nothing happens, patients in some countries lack medicine, others buy it cheaply.
madam president, i supported this report on the strategy for the integration of the roma population. i think integration needs to be promoted, and i also agree with the main goals of integration, which are education, health, employment and housing. the european parliament has added the issue of non-discrimination, which is of course very important to find funds, projects, programmes to support the integration of roma. and also to use these resources effectively, i.e. to control the use and also to control the development of indicators. i also welcome the initiative to involve representatives of the roma minority itself in the development of national roma integration strategies. ladies and gentlemen, with regard to the integration of roma in the czech republic, i have a great deal of experience in supporting the education of roma children, i know that this is problematic, but i know that this is the main way to their successful and long-term integration.
madam president, i have supported this report and i would also like to say that i am very pleased that it has been tabled and that it has been voted on by a very substantial majority. i fully share the view that no one should feel excluded, ostracized or simply unable to make a difference. i support the goals that have been set out here, whether it's the goal of radically improving the education of the roma community, of course the issue of employment is very pressing in many states as is the issue of housing. in general, non-discrimination must be applied to every community, to every section of society. at the same time, i would like to say that this group is facing new challenges, of course, we need to connect all the actors and strategies to help this group. i think they're known, they're described. it's just a matter of getting synergies. and this group should be aware that there are not only rights but also duties that they should respect in a meaningful way and that their representatives should also lead them to do so.
madam president, the protection of human rights is one of the fundamental values on which the european union is founded. the adoption of the charter of fundamental rights of the european union and its binding nature thus represents a major step by which the european union has sent a signal that the protection of human rights is and will always be the guiding principle for all institutions, bodies and member states in the application of union law. the observance of the charter must be unconditional. however, i would be very careful to interpret it as a strategic document to which the institutions' internal rules should be adapted. institutions must be transparent, but if we introduce in the name of the charter continuous human rights impact assessments, numerous training and other bureaucratic processes, then we end up as an administrative colossus in which the real meaning of the charter as a catalogue of human rights is drowned. that is why i abstained in the final vote.
madam president, i have supported this report on the sustainable use of pesticides. i agree that member states should correct their shortcomings without further delay. i agree that we should encourage research into the secondary effects of pesticides. i also support investments in research to develop safer and more affordable alternatives to current pesticides. all this is very important, as is the availability of alternatives, to maintain the competitiveness of european agriculture. as regards their use, pesticides contain components which are highly hazardous to humans and the environment. it is necessary to prohibit strictly the use of these substances, including the importation of banned pesticides into the european union from third countries.
madam president, i too supported this report and i would like to state in advance that the long-term, mass use of pesticides has been controversial for many years. it's very good that we've taken this step today, because this massive use of pesticides has a staggering impact on the environment. i've recorded a report that says that by the end of the century, virtually all insects will be gone. it's really incredible the effects that use of these substances, which are very sensitive to these organisms, and i'm not talking about the effects on humans, of course. we need to invest in research and innovation. it's our future, because this pollution of nature has really reached massive proportions. i would also like to point out that it is the environmental approach and the sustainability of the environmental approach that we should be reflecting in the other subsidy flows. we should also adapt our reform of the common agricultural policy to this. sustainability must be our long-term goal.

for the group gue/ngl. mr president, bosnia and herzegovina is one of the former yugoslav republics with the most complex population structure. in addition to the wide range of balkan nationalities, a distinctive religious structure has developed over the centuries. in the cities, muslims dominated, in the countryside, orthodox. a small area in the northeast of the country has traditionally been populated by catholics. so-called serbs, so-called bosnians and so-called croats all speak three dialects of a common language, regardless of religion. after a bloody war supported by outside forces, two structural units were established in the territory. one of them in the northeast republic of serbia and the other is the muslim-croatian federation. it is completely incomprehensible to me that the european union has been trying to change this situation from the beginning, despite the wishes of the people of the country. the dayton peace agreement does provide for certain procedures and it is not possible to require a change in the constitutional order as part of the negotiating conditions for any country's accession to the european union. bosnia and herzegovina has a number of problems that are far more important to the lives of the local population. first of all, it is about connecting energy networks with neighbouring countries, improving rail and road infrastructure and reaching a clear agreement on the rules of coexistence with important neighbours, in particular serbia and, as my colleague mentioned, croatia. as for the constant use of the dog's head by the leadership of the republic of serbia, the cover-up of war crimes committed by croatian and muslim forces, including the so-called srebrenica massacre, then these efforts are deeply troubling me. the european report is unbalanced, i cannot support this wording.

mr president, i too want to express my belief in trade without barriers, that is to say, free trade, which is of course based on common values. i think we share a number of common values with singapore, whether it's support for a liberal-conservative orientation of the state, the rule of law, trade that is based on fair principles. i want to commend the reporter, his negotiating team. i think that the mutual respect of both parties has found a balanced position in the negotiated agreement. and i think these are opportunities first of all for our businesses, opportunities for our citizens, of course, institutions. i don't know what other agreement would be on the table if there are repeated objections, there is no other agreement on the table, we are losing time only because of the eu institutions' action. i'm fully in favor of this motion.
member of the commission. – mr president, let me answer a few questions. public debt is excluded from this. the investment protection agreement (ipa) is very clear on excluding the eu sovereign debt restructuring from the investment protection, so here you can be calm. investor-state dispute settlement (isds) does not exist anymore in the european union. we have replaced it – very much at your demand and with your help, cooperation and push, and i thank you for that – with a new modern system that the advocate general declared perfectly compatible with eu laws. it does away with many of the flaws of isds. there are full—term arbitrators with a code of conduct, there is full transparency, there is an appeal mechanism, there are very clear limits on what can be brought to the table, and it says that states have the right to regulate. singapore is not on the list of non—cooperative jurisdictions for tax evasion. on geographical indications (gis), it is true that this still remains to be done. singapore is about to establish a register to protect the gis to which the european union countries can apply for protection of the 196 geographical indications that were on the consulting list and the free trade agreement (fta) will enter into force only when there is satisfactory compliance with this. it is true that singapore still has to ratify three ilo conventions, but hard work is ongoing inside the government of singapore and cooperation with the ilo to make sure that legislation is in compliance with these three conventions and that they are eventually ratified. as was referred to, there has been a letter from the government of singapore on this process. if – and i hope you will – this house votes strongly in favour of these agreements tomorrow, you will vote for an ambitious fta, which will be a reference for many other upcoming agreements in the asean region. you will vote in favour of an investment protection agreement that follows the approach that you have helped to shape. you will also help to frame the broader cooperation between the eu and singapore, giving our relations a solid and broad foundation in areas such as security, people—to—people contact, research etc., through the partnership and cooperation agreement and it will be the first agreement that is endorsed by this parliament following the new modern architecture. with this we are expanding the circle of friends that we are setting up with like-minded countries, countries who believe in multilateralism, countries who believe in global rules and norms, and countries who believe that we can make mutually beneficial, fair and sustainable trade agreements with friends and with whom we are contributing to shaping globalisation. thank you very much for this debate and thank you again to the two rapporteurs.

the next item is the debate on the oral question to the commission on policy challenges and strategies against women’s cancers and related comorbidities, by daniela aiuto, on behalf of the committee on women’s rights and gender equality (o-000134/2018 – b8—0006/2019) (2018/2782(rsp).
madam president, the statistics are clear, they are unrelenting, it is simply that cancer is a killer and that in the eu up to 26% of deaths are caused by cancer. fortunately, the european union recognises the potential for cooperation between member states and has come up with a number of initiatives to address it. i would like to thank the commissioner for listing them here, and he did so with great personal interest. it's clear that he cares a lot about this and that he understands this work that he's doing for the citizens, and especially for the women. breast cancer is one of the most common cancers in the eu. it accounts for 13.4% of all cancers. in the prevention of cancer in women, healthy lifestyles, healthy diets, and of course screening and diagnosis, must be promoted. i hope that the european social fund, which will integrate the existing health programme, will also help to improve the situation.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i agree that this is an important piece of legislation, which addresses some of the concerns currently related to investments coming to sensitive sectors in europe. these concerns exist, various member states face them and i am convinced that we can now bring a new instrument to our toolbox in this area. for instance, if we had this regulation in place today, we could have come up with a more coordinated approach to some of the chinese companies, huawei for instance. personally, i value in this legislation the exchange of information between respective stakeholders in the member states. some of the potentially problematic investments could come to several countries, and it would be beneficial to share information on them. it remains on a voluntary basis to set up a screening mechanism at a national level, but there is no doubt that there are good reasons to control investments coming to very sensitive areas such as critical infrastructure, defence and the security industry, but also the media or – very topical now – telecommunications and new digital technologies, such as artificial intelligence. the eu needs to remain open to foreign investment. it has always been a source of our economic growth. through the process of negotiating this regulation, i emphasise that this is by no means a protectionist tool, but a guarantee for the eu to face those investments where economic goals are not a primary consideration, a sort of warning in the same way that we need trade, defence or anti-dumping legislation. they are triggered only when a threat is identified. i believe this regulation will prove useful and functional once applied.

vice-president of the commission. – madam president, my thanks to you and to the honourable members for your interventions and for your cooperation and support to achieve such a good result on this proposal. i very much welcome the agreement between parliament and the council, because mutual recognition is at the core of single markets for goods. but it is still one of the most important remaining barriers. with regard to the broader questions as to whether the regulation is impacting member states’ capacity to protect legitimate public objectives, the answer is no. mutual recognition is not automatic and therefore cannot jeopardise legitimate public objectives. this is embedded in the principle itself. protection of legitimate public interests will always remain the exception to the mutual recognition law provided that it’s justified and proportionate. to conclude, with your vote tomorrow you are sending a strong and clear message about delivering on our commitment to help businesses trade in a single market. our smes will benefit and our citizens will benefit.
vice-president of the commission. – madam president, first of all, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, eva maydell, on her strong will and ambition, ensuring that parliament could form its views quickly and effectively, which led to the text you will vote on tomorrow. we welcome parliament’s strong commitment to deepening the single market for retail financial services and payments, and to making the most of the euro, the eu’s common currency – for the benefit of citizens and businesses outside as well as inside the euro area. today, only euro-area citizens enjoy the benefits of cheap euro transactions. around 150 million europeans who live in eu countries using other currencies do not. in fact, they sometimes have to pay very high and disproportionate fees for such transactions, particularly in cross-border situations. let me give you an example. imagine that you live in italy and you wish to send eur 50 to finland. because these countries are both in the euro area, eu rules prevent banks from charging more for this transaction than they would for sending money, say, from rome to milan. now, imagine instead that you are living in bulgaria and you want to transfer the same eur 50 to finland. because domestic payments in euro in bulgaria are generally expensive, the equivalent cross-border transaction is also very expensive. for a bulgarian citizen this means he or she could end up paying as much as eur 24 for this kind of transfer. the revised cross-border payments regulation will remove this unequal treatment by extending the benefits of low-cost euro transactions across the whole eu. the regulation will ensure that, by 15 december this year, all european citizens and businesses will have the right to make cross-border payments in euros for the same price as domestic payments in their national currency. the cross-border payments regulation will positively affect the vast majority – around 80% – of cross-border transactions originating from non-euro member states and made in euros. putting an end to high fees linked to cross-border euro payments will enable consumers and businesses in non-euro member states to save around eur 900 million every year. this will also contribute to our common objective of strengthening the international role of the euro as a payments currency. the revised cross-border payments regulation will also increase the transparency of currency conversion services. this is especially important for several million europeans who travel within the eu every year, either for business or for pleasure. often, when paying by card or withdrawing cash at an atm in a country that uses a different currency from the euro, it is very difficult to know exactly how much it will cost. it is equally difficult for consumers to choose the most convenient option when they are given the choice, on the spot, between paying in their own currency or in the local currency, for example when paying a hotel bill. by harmonising the disclosure of currency conversion costs, the regulation will allow european consumers to check and compare the currency conversion charges applied by their bank or by merchants. consumers will be able to compare charges when shopping abroad with their cards, when withdrawing cash at an atm abroad and when sending money, from their own country’s banking platforms, to a member state using a different eu currency. this will boost competition between providers in such currency conversion services and should thus lower the cost of transactions between member states in different currencies. honourable members, the european project is quite often perceived as distant from the lives of european citizens. this legislative initiative is a good opportunity to demonstrate how europe can bring tangible benefits to its citizens.
on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, firstly, let me thank the rapporteur for her excellent work on this proposal, which is a real example of eu regulation working to cut costs and burdens, and putting the consumer first. the regulation ensures that the costs for cross-border payments in euro from one eu country to another are the same as an equivalent domestic payment, regardless of whether the countries are in the eurozone or not. furthermore, customers in the eu withdrawing money from an atm in a foreign currency will have much more clarity on the cost of their payment options. following pressure from parliament, the charges associated with credit transfers will also be made clear to consumers. from parliament’s perspective, full comparability is the ultimate goal. that means that customers should always be informed of the cost of their payments in a foreign currency within the eu. the final text of this regulation takes a significant step towards full comparability but, in my view, more can be done. therefore, i welcome the review that the commission will undertake three years after the regulation enters into force. the review should explore how costs to consumers can be reduced further and should ensure full comparability of payment options. nonetheless, customers will soon begin to reap the benefits of the new provisions, and i’m proud of the work we have done here in parliament to bring this about.
