rapporteur. mr president, mr commissioner, mr president of the court of auditors, allow me to begin by thanking my colleagues, the court of auditors, the commission representatives and, of course, the representatives of the joint undertakings. i am very pleased that we have been able to evaluate the 2017 financial year together, not only from a purely financial point of view, but also from the point of view of real results and impacts. i appreciate this cooperation, which for me started last year when i was the shadow joint venture newsreader. i consider the evaluation of the management and performance of joint ventures to be very important, as in many cases they already affect our lives. whether it is the simple spending side of the eu budget, or the pooling and activation of private capital and the resulting employment, or the development of science and research. the total budget of the joint undertakings for 2017 was eur 2,1 billion, representing around 2% of the total eu budget for that year. the joint undertakings employed almost 700 staff in 2017. the joint undertakings aim to carry out research and innovation activities, to increase the competitiveness of european industry and to address major societal challenges. joint undertakings thus play a key role in connecting key strategic partners from industry and science, contributing to closer cooperation between actors in different sectors and to the creation of long-term cooperation networks. it is the linking of the public and private sectors within these enterprises that is the effective way to develop european research and to finance it effectively. e eu joint undertakings show that if we effectively link not only european money but also private capital and the knowledge and skills of citizens, the european union can be a strong global player in research and innovation. i also see the importance of the participation of smes in the activities of the joint undertakings. i am pleased that the joint undertakings are trying to support them and i hope that they will participate in their activities on an even larger scale in the future. i am very positive that horizon 2020 also includes key performance indicators. the joint undertakings should be congratulated in this respect, as most of them met or exceeded these key performance indicators set for the entire programming period in 2017. the efficiency of the joint undertakings is further underlined by the fact that the management cost ratio remains below 5%, which indicates their lean and efficient organisational structure. i also welcome the fact that, in the case of joint undertakings, the vast majority of calls for proposals were published and closed in accordance with the relevant work plans and that the granting and disbursement periods were well below the defined targets. the commission will continue to monitor the implementation of the joint undertaking's activities and will continue to monitor the implementation of the iter joint undertaking's activities. only in the case of the ecsel joint undertaking did the court of auditors issue a qualified opinion on the legality and regularity of the payments. however, this is the result of projects taken over from the legal predecessors of the ecsel joint undertaking, i.e. the artemis and eniac joint undertakings, and concerns unfinished projects of the previous framework programme. so once again, let me thank all those involved for their cooperation and congratulate the joint undertakings on the results presented. in accordance with the unanimous decision of the committee on budgetary control, i also ask the european parliament's plenary to approve the annual accounts for 2017 of all eight joint undertakings.
the commission's annual accounts for the financial year 2000 are the basis for the annual accounts of the european commission for the financial year 2000 and the annual accounts of the european commission for the financial year 2000. i would like to thank the rapporteur, inés ayal sender, not only for her exemplary cooperation but above all for continuing the process of changing the perception of discharge as a meaningful part of the life cycle of the management of the funds of the citizens of the european union that i started in 2015. while errors or omissions need to be found, corrected and mechanisms put in place to prevent them, it is also very important to stress that errors represent less than 3% of the union's financial expenditure. we should not forget that more than 97% of the expenditure that has served its purpose has been used properly and in a timely manner, not only for the benefit of individual citizens and businesses, but also for the benefit of our common objectives. it is these achievements that we must take as an example for the future and present to the citizens of the union as the success of the european project. as rapporteur for the european commission's 2014 discharge main report, i have already worked to ensure that discharge is seen as a meaningful part of the life cycle of the european union's citizens' spending. so i'm pleased that the focus on performance and error prevention is becoming a bigger part of the report. in conclusion, i would like to ask that we focus our budgetary assessment on the facts and context of the financial year in question, and not use the year in question as a fictitious guide to future events that are unrelated and have no place or justification in today's debate.
mr president, commissioner, who is not currently listening, mr president of the european court of auditors, i am personally pleased to be able to support the discharge for all the joint undertakings and to highlight the balanced reports presented. cooperation through research and development joint ventures is essential for the european union, especially for the development of our know-how in the field of innovation and technology. the strategy at eu level gives a much clearer and stronger mandate to member states to strengthen and improve cooperation between businesses across the european union. however, allow me, commissioner, who is not listening, to ask you at the discharge the fundamental questions that we are facing today, and that is the conflict of interest. we have a pretty basic policy that was supposed to address this, and it was bart staes who asked you three important questions. let me have one too. i want to ask you, commissioner, if you are listening to me, if you are really going to address the conflicts of interest of some of the top politicians and if you will answer us why some of your questions sent to the prime ministers are still secret.
mr president, commissioner, president of the court of auditors, first of all i would like to thank the rapporteur, mrs dlabaj, for her excellent cooperation and for the preparation of the corrective reports. this year i was the joint venture's accounting officer for the fourth consecutive year. and i would like to say that there is a gradual improvement in both project management and the internal organisation and control systems of these companies. there are some risks. the joint undertakings have not yet caught up or overcome the initial delay in the implementation of horizon 2020, but i still believe that quality is more important than quantity and that the european budget is best served by properly invested resources. however, the accounts have confirmed the high european added value of the activities of these joint undertakings and i am pleased to be able to recommend the discharge for all of them for the financial year 2017.

president of the european court of auditors. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. first of all, i would like to thank you very much for the interesting debate and the many suggestions. i would like to limit myself to two aspects, however, in the light of the time. the first concerns the issue of conflicts of interest and ethical issues. i wanted to inform mrs macovei that it is our intention - and we are currently in the middle of it, and the committee responsible is dealing with this - to complete and present a report on the state of the ethical rules in the institutions before the summer break. we are also subjecting ourselves to a peer review process which will be completed well before the summer break. the second aspect i would like to mention is the desire, which has been repeatedly expressed, for even more error rates in even more sectors of the budget. i want to make this very clear: that will not be possible. that doesn't make any sense either. what's the error rate? the error rate is a statistical-mathematical method that we use to calculate a certain probability for a certain error rate by testing a thousand transactions across the entire european budget -- about a thousand transactions -- and then, based on the error results that we see in doing that, we do a probability calculation where we said last year, 2017, the probable error rate, the estimated error rate is 2.4 percent with a 95 percent probability and a tolerance rate of plus one percent and minus one percent. in plain language, it is somewhere between 1.4 and 3.4 percent. and if we now break this down into smaller areas and work on smaller areas with, of course, a much smaller amount of data, then the accuracy of this error rate gets smaller and smaller. this means that if we find, for example, in the administrative area, an error rate of 0.9 or 0.5, then this is a pure estimate, and the probable error rate can be above or below. and in this statistical field, it simply no longer makes sense to try to make more precise statements, because the effort you have to make is out of proportion to what is at stake. we're doing about 40 to 50 percent of what we can do with our resources today. there's not much more that's gonna be possible. and i think that i have to say that at this point, it is also in response to those repeated requests that we should do more, more precisely and in more areas. it doesn't work, it doesn't make any mathematical-statistical sense, because we just can't get the accuracy we need.
member of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. i should like to make a brief comment on three politically important points or statements. firstly, on the specific questions concerning conflict of interest and the czech republic: since the beginning of january, we have been working with teams of experts from different directorates-general - dg regio, dg agri and dg employment - and other experts on the ground in prague to review the documents, to collect figures, data and processes. we're in the middle of a full evaluation with our experts. i have asked my colleagues and also the directors-general to do this as intensively as necessary and as promptly as possible. i can't give you a time limit. this is a case where thoroughness is more important than speed. but rest assured, we're on the move. i want to give the committee on budgetary control a comprehensive interim report on the audits, the first results of the evaluation and also on possible conclusions in a few weeks' time, as agreed with your committee secretariat, here in strasbourg on monday 15 april. i shall be there, without the time constraint that exists today, ready to give a full answer to all questions. secondly, the vacancy of a director-general or senior manager versus transfer. again, we are convinced that both ways, instruments, of arriving at a decision on an item are equally valid in our legal frameworks. i 'd like to add this . what are the reasons for a transfer? there are two reasons that might be quite interesting. firstly, there are more transfers at the beginning of a commission's term of office, but only at the beginning. a new commission has new directorates-general, reassigns directors-general and therefore also comes to the conclusion that the pool of competent senior managers should be reorganized. this only makes sense by transfer. and secondly, we do not practice as a rigid rule, but as an important part of our personnel management, mobility after five or six years of changing from one office to another. again, this is only by transfer. i remember that there have been complaints from this house on a number of occasions when directors-general or similar positions have been occupied for six, seven, eight, nine years by the same woman, by the same man. we were asked why you didn't regroup. it's only a transfer. we will follow up on this, but it is clear - and i shall continue to make this point in the coming days - that the tendering procedure and thus the competition is the most obvious procedure. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i quote my statement from march in the committee on budgetary control word for word. i quote it because many questions required input from a subject and we wanted to answer as fully as possible. he did not do this to influence the wording but to complete the content. for example, one of the questions asked by your house was, "how long did mr selmayr know about this?" if a question of yours had been, in the course of 2015, 2016, 2017, 2018, january, february? when did he know? it was clear then that this question and others had to be answered only with him or even only by him. if we had answered, we are sorry, we do not know, we have a secretary-general who is in the same building, one floor higher than us, but we cannot give an answer. i would like to hear your response. that's why we brought him in. he was present, as far as he and only he could give a concrete answer to many questions for your high house.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, i would like to thank honourable members so much for this useful exchange of views. i am glad to hear that the council and parliament share many concerns, as well the common wish to see year-on-year improvements, creating the basis for an unqualified audit opinion from the court. all parties are encouraged to work together towards sound financial management and control of eu funds. it’s very important that we show our determination and urge all actors to fully assume their responsibilities. progress needs to be achieved in the coming years. but overall i think the message sent by this debate to europe’s citizens and taxpayers is clear: we are serious and thorough in our scrutiny of eu spending and we want to make sure that the eu budget is on track in delivering its expected results.

for the group gue/ngl. mr president, commissioner, if i were to take account purely of the outcome of the negotiations in this document, perhaps i could say that i am satisfied because, as my colleagues have said, it was a tough negotiation and certainly a lot of good came out of it, for which i owe a special thanks to miriam dalli. but the overwhelming demands on manufacturers to develop new technology were passed on, and that was the end of it. we're already short of the b. the new version of the clean and energy efficient road vehicles directive, which was supposed to kick-start the clean vehicle market, is simply not ambitious, public sector purchases are too late and the resulting obligations are full of loopholes. and so i ask myself: who will the car manufacturers sell to, if the public sector is not willing to buy them? and how will the manufacturers meet the targets we've agreed? the member states have simply washed their hands of the solution, leaving the burden solely on european products. the infrastructure for clean vehicles is still missing, and we're in a vicious circle of needing to start a market for these cars, but no one is willing to buy them yet because of the missing infrastructure, and no one is willing to build them until there's a greater proliferation of these cars. fellas, don't be mad at me, but we may have taken the right first step, but it's just an open pandora's box, because all the other steps we were supposed to take, and it was supposed to be a package, we failed. and if we're going to say that this one step is going to save something, then i'm telling you that this decision is wrong.
mr president, climate change is making citizens and politicians more sensitive to the environment, and rightly so. transport, of course, by its emissions of carbon dioxide and other substances, contributes to this problem, that is, to climate change. but i would caution against the irresponsible pursuit of ambitious limits. first, because the european union is not a decisive producer of co2, it accounts for less than 10% of global production. the second reason is that the automotive industry is a key player in the european union and we simply cannot subject it to unrealistic targets, unrealistic pressure on its competitiveness. we could totally destroy it, eliminate jobs, and we wouldn't actually be reducing co2 as much as we'd like. i was happy with the european commission's proposal, and i was happy with the 15% by 2025, but i am not happy with the ambitious increase by 2030 proposed by the trialogue agreement.

madam president, i see the neighbourhood policy and development aid as one of the instruments of our security. we can't just worry about security in our own space, but we have to focus on the world around us. there's no doubt about that. i therefore welcome the amount proposed to be budgeted under this new instrument. in my view, this instrument promotes a higher level of efficiency precisely by merging several previously separate instruments. i very much hope that there will be a reduction in administrative and personnel costs, for example, and that the efficiency will really show. we're here every thursday to look at human rights abuses in many countries around the world. and i think that we can now, with the political conditionality, that is, by fulfilling the basic democratic procedures, those that we think are the content of our europeanism, we can make that development aid conditional on that, and i think that can also contribute to a greater effectiveness of our policy.
for the group gue/ngl. madam president, in november 2015 the european commission set out a medium-term strategy for the eu's enlargement policy. gradually, all the western balkan countries have been granted candidate status and are currently engaged in a long-term dialogue with a view to eu membership on the basis of the copenhagen criteria. this strategy is to be strengthened in npp iii, which will enter into force in 2021, and is to maximise the impact of accession negotiations and facilitate the transition process of individual states. for all countries, the emphasis is on an economic reform agenda, the stability of institutions guaranteeing democracy, the rule of law, human rights and respect for and protection of minorities, the existence of a functioning market economy and the ability to cope with competitive pressures and market forces within the eu. the total amount proposed is eur 14,5 billion for the period 2021-2027 to provide financing for pre-accession assistance. this amount is comparable in annual terms to the czech republic's annual contribution to the eu budget. the programme will seek a wide range of applications in the areas of internal policies, security, migration, research and innovation, environment and climate. the erasmus+ programme will certainly play a significant role. unfortunately, the whole regulation allows the european commission to enter the programme in a fundamental way on the basis of not entirely clear criteria. this is also why the gue/ngl group welcomes the whole programme on the one hand, but cannot unambiguously support it on the other.
i think, madam president, that the countries which are really seeking to join the european union need our help. they should feel it, and we are also following up the debate on the european neighbourhood policy, which was discussed in the previous paragraph. i believe it is essential that pre-accession financial support to countries should be motivating. to actually lead to support for legislative reform in these states. at the same time, i must stress that there is a good proposal to strengthen the role of the european parliament as a directly elected body, which will actually increase the use of the procedure in delegated acts. i really appreciate that. i would also like to comment specifically on turkey's role in the pre-accession assistance. i would like the people of turkey to feel the support of the union. however, turkey today, in my opinion, is not ready to join the european union. and not only the member states, but i think the turkish government is aware of that. there is a need for dialogue with turkey, but there are fundamental violations of our democratic values, and i think that is the clear answer to whether turkey is ready or not.

mr. chairman, water is life. it is a strategic resource and we must take better care of it, because even in the european union there are already countries, regions, where water is scarce, where drinking water has to be brought from a greater distance. water is indeed becoming a strategic resource, and i therefore welcome the european directive which is revising our approach. after 20 years, this amendment is certainly necessary. i therefore support all four priority areas for improvement as outlined by the rapporteur here, whether it be risk assessment, enhancing transparency, access to information and the quality of water supply materials. the tightening of the values of some indicators is of course necessary, but they cannot be set without reason to increase the price of drinking water, and this must of course be sufficiently subjected to scientific research. however, health comes first, and we must approach it that way. implementation and responsibility for water quality remain with the member states and the principle of subsidiarity is therefore respected. this must also be applied to ensuring access to water. i also consider it important to ensure transparency and to provide sufficient information to consumers. finally, i would like to call for the careful use of drinking water and to point out that the structural funds of the european union have also made a significant contribution to improving the management of waste water in the new member states. so water resources and water purity must be protected for the health of all of us and for the future.
for the ppe group. mr president, commissioner, i would like to congratulate my colleague, angelica niebler, on her excellent report. preventive restructuring as such is of course a very important tool for european entrepreneurs. by removing national differences in preventive restructuring rules, we will promote the application of the fundamental freedoms of free movement of capital or freedom of establishment. simplifying the rules will also support smes, which represent 99% of all businesses in the union, which often lack the resources to handle complex cross-border procedures. the proposal therefore brings a clear union added value. the path of minimum harmonisation creates sufficient scope for member states to apply the directive according to their own needs while fulfilling its essential objectives. the debtor-creditor relationship often raises complex issues, not only of commercial relations but also of human rights and human dignity. in the czech republic, this is a very sensitive issue. i know very well from my own country's experience that not all debtors can be seen as someone who needs to be taught a lesson in recklessness. but i also know that creditors must be allowed to effectively recover their debts. in this respect, i think the report represents a good compromise, or rather, it contains an approach which reflects both sides of the coin, the relationship between creditor and debtor. i therefore fully support this report and will vote in favour of its adoption.

mr president, i would like to point out that the crossborder distribution of collective investment funds has a clear pro-european legislative reason. we sometimes vote on legislation here which i do not think is entirely consistent with what the european treaties say can be regulated at european level. but the cross-border element in the distribution of collective investment funds is unquestionable. we have adopted amendments that establish a level playing field for collective investment undertakings, of course removing the obstacles to the free movement of units and shares of collective investment funds. the aim is to create a more uniform protection for investors, but there are still obstacles to managers of these funds taking advantage of this very freedom of movement. so i am glad that we have made this progress, it is a europe without barriers that i support, and i am glad that we have succeeded in accomplishing this task in this election period.
mr president, i voted in favour of this report, and i will explain my vote here. we also dealt with the consequences of the financial crisis of 2007-2008, when not all the problems associated with the stabilisation of the european banking system or, in general, with the reduction of risks in the financial sector, were solved. we've been dealing with the issues of counterparty credit risk, leverage and net stable funding. i naturally agree with the amendments adopted by the european parliament. this is the disclosure and reporting of information within entities operating in the financial sector. i would like to emphasize the scope of the leverage that this legislation brings. it is based in particular on not having too much leverage in the period of upswing, which i think is a very positive measure and that is why i supported this report.
mr president, i am pleased that this directive has finally passed with a large majority. of course, predictable and transparent working conditions are very important for employees, but also for employers to be sure what information they have to provide to their prospective and existing employees, and in what timeframe. so the trialogue agreement is, i think, relatively balanced for both employees and employers, which is why i endorsed it. in particular, workers will be informed earlier about their rights and should also have easier access to stable employment. the final text respects national legislation, which can better take account of the national specificities of the labour market, and should also not lead to an undue bureaucratic burden for employers, which i consider very important.
mr president, i have finally voted in favour of this regulation establishing a new labour authority. although i am not in favour of the creation of new bodies, i supported this proposal because it is necessary to monitor the implementation of european law on the internal market in matters of posting of workers, compliance with legislation concerning the employment of persons, and so on. i think it is important that this body, which has remained a body, although it is more of an agency, does not overlap with other competences of other agencies. the implementation and supervision of the european parliament will be important here. i would very much like the seat of this new agency to be in a new member state, preferably in slovakia. i think it would be a very affordable and good solution.
mr president, social policy is still a crucial domain for national governments as well as social systems. i therefore did not support this text, not only because i am opposed to the creation of any new european agencies or institutions. i also did not support this text because i am afraid that such an agency would try to interfere with national labour law and also influence social systems. that is something which, in my view, would be outside the scope of european law, because it is not really a matter for the european union. we have 28 economies here, soon to be 27 economies, that are different, we have countries that are relatively rich and relatively poor, and it's not possible to try to smooth them into one legal framework in this way. that's why i couldn't support this text.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i will refrain from repeating what you have said, because i think that a lot of what has been said is a reality and is something that one can subscribe to. i think that it is correct to say that we have made progress. it is also correct to say, as the commissioner has said, that there is still an effort to be done, by all means. but, in order not to be repetitive, let me say that while all that has been said with regard to the platforms is definitely relevant and there is still a lot of room to cover, there are also other aspects that we need to be aware of. i need to draw a parallel with the recent negotiations on the cyberecurity act, because, after all, elections and our sovereignty are key, but also the health and lives of our people, are equally key. the fact, madam minister, is that regarding what you have said just a few minutes ago – as relevant as it is – i don’t see the backing in a number of member states for that. i don’t see the recognition on the side of a number of member states on different levels that we are facing the threat that some of the colleagues have already spoken about, whether it is the question of european elections and the other elections, lives or safety. we even see trojan horses in the european union; that is a reality. so i think it is fair to say that, while we have managed to progress both on the legislation as well as what you have said with regard to our safety, but also with regard to the european elections, what we really need – and the commission knows my point of view on that, because we were in a debate just some weeks ago – is a real mindset change, because this is a reality. we are confronting a war which is led against us – not by means of arms, but something as dangerous as that – and i think that unless we recognise it and unless we are consistent in our attention and our efforts – and not just before european elections – we will be hit, we will be vulnerable and we will be damaged.
blue-card question. – i would definitely agree with you on the question of freedom of expression, but i did not detect in your remarks any concern regarding, let’s say, possible cyber-attacks from the east or elsewhere with interference in european any other elections. so my question is: do you share that concern, and if you do, how would you tackle it in terms of means to confront that risk and how would you minimise it?
mr. chairman, ladies and gentlemen, this debate is nice, it's nice in how we all want to do more to make this election, i would say, neutral in the world of cybersecurity. but i would like to say one thing: is this debate too late? is this debate just an exchange of views, i would say, on how we should have dealt with the various actors who were influencing important developments in the european union? i too welcome the package prepared by the european commission, but, commissioner, i am telling you, we are not going to protect these elections in the same special way with this package. there is a need to work systematically, even after you are no longer commissioner, on further measures to ensure that no elections, no referendums and no other acts of democracy are influenced by anyone. there's been talk across the political spectrum about how we want to fight the spread of fake news, but the only way to fight fake news is to have educated citizens who aren't afraid to check information and try to find the truth. i challenge you, my colleagues, to be informed citizens who seek the truth.
member of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, the last time i had the honour to address this plenary was on 11 december last year, and at the time i welcomed the political consensus that emerged at the itre committee – thank you, president buzek – and the ability of going at speed and quality that was the ambition of parliament to agree on the framework programme regulation establishing horizon europe and a specific programme decision, thus opening the way for the council and the commission to start the real negotiations. and here we are again, four months and three trilogues later, and i’m very happy. very happy that we reached such a well-balanced agreement on horizon europe. congratulations. congratulations, mr nica; congratulations mr ehler, for the great work achieved. it was really impressive how you did it, how you achieved a consensus, how you worked so hard, and how everyone worked so hard. so, really, congratulations and i’ll be watching tomorrow on this confirmation. i wanted also to thank president buzek, who was also always there with us, in the itre committee. congratulations to the council; congratulations to the romanian presidency; and – i wanted to be a little bit personal – congratulations, ambassador, to you, and to your team. you worked very hard, 24 hours a day, to achieve this and i’m really, deeply thankful for that. (applause)last week, i had a beautiful moment. i think we all had a beautiful moment – to have for the first time in our lifetime a picture of a black hole. imagine that! a man 100 years ago, albert einstein, imagined a black hole, and for 100 years people tried to achieve and to prove that that was right, and we did it. we did it, and europe was there, touching the button of that picture, which will make for the world things that we cannot even imagine, because even einstein could never have imagined what he did: how much that would impact the world. i think there was one scientist there that said, ‘you know, there’s a history of science before that moment, before the picture, and there will be a history of science after that moment’. i think that, for me, if i could have described european science it was that moment. because that moment was about open science, about open innovation, and about openness to the world: in that room that day, they were representing 200 scientists. they were representing 40 different nationalities. and so i’m extremely proud, because horizon europe is just about that. it is about our leadership as europeans in science. when you think about everything that we have achieved: that we have decided that on the next programme we will have these missions that mr ehler referred to: missions of science that people will understand. that we will communicate with people about the things that we want to do – to cure diseases like cancer. that will link us to the people. and then the ways that we will do it in terms of the european innovation council and the way we will innovate on the financing and the funding for innovation. the partnerships where we decided to go for an easier way, for a less complex way of defining those partnerships. and, of course, one of the points that was so important for all of us is that while the programme is about excellence, we have also to help those that are going up that ladder of excellence, and that we owe to you. we owe it to you, to the parliament, that we achieved that agreement; that we will put 3.3% of the budget for widening measures in the future. that is a big achievement, and i also wanted to congratulate you for that. so, by agreeing on horizon europe, we have sent a strong signal to the mff negotiators about the importance of science and innovation, and it is now our common task to defend a robust budget for it. this will be the next challenge, my friends, and i count on all of you to make sure that horizon europe will become not only the biggest science and innovation programme ever, but also the one delivering the highest impact for our citizens.
the rapporteur of the committee on budgetary control. mr president, in today's rapidly globalising world, it is essential to focus on the results of future research and innovation in the european union. compared to other large economies, europe suffers from a lack of funding, even though research and innovation are the basis for sustainable jobs and growth. the current horizon 2020 programme has already delivered a unique european added value by promoting competitiveness and cooperation for the best scientific knowledge and innovation across the continent. from the budgetary control point of view, there is a continuing need to increase synergies between research and innovation programmes and other european union programmes, to continue simplifying rules and procedures and to better inform europeans about the results of research and innovation. horizon europe fulfils this by focusing on excellence, transnational competition and cooperation. i believe it will meet those expectations and have an even greater impact than the current program. in conclusion, let me thank commissioner moedas for his always constructive cooperation with the committee on budgetary control.
mr president, first of all i would like to thank the two rapporteurs, dan nic and christian ehler, for their tremendous commitment and excellent cooperation with us, the shadow rapporteurs. thank you very much, and i appreciate it very much. the amount of assigned revenue in accordance with article 21 (3) of the financial regulation is estimated at eur 100000. i'm very glad that even though we had little time, we came to a balanced conclusion. i am also pleased that my colleagues have accepted my proposal to include support for research in the so-called coal regions. i consider this essential for the regulation and the objectives we have set ourselves in the field of energy in the european union. horizon will also help to promote the sharing of experience of the economic and social changes associated with the decline of coal mining. i also consider the area of the so-called widening countries to be very important. we need to focus on a more balanced recruitment and remuneration of scientists across the european union in the future. only then can we realize their full potential and prevent them from leaving for other scientific centers around the world.
member of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, first of all i would like to thank the european parliament, and in particular the rapporteur, mrs schaldemose, but also the fictitious rapporteurs, for all their efforts and cooperation in reaching agreement on this matter in such a short time. parliament has helped to strengthen and shape the text of the new regulation. your amendments will help create a fairer environment for businesses in the online platform economy. this will benefit the consumers who use these platforms. there will be a clear set of rules and a more predictable regulatory environment. this in turn will build trust across borders. we would obviously like this to help platforms grow, invest and innovate. some of these additional provisions proposed by the european parliament will help more than one million european smes, which offer their goods and services via online platforms, to reach their customers. let me highlight the exploitation systems, where parliament has filled a potential gap. it was clarified that online intermediation services cannot evade their obligations when controlling the underlying operating systems. i also welcome the introduction of a list of specific contractual terms and conditions to ensure fair treatment between online intermediation services and their business users and to prohibit certain harmful practices. this will make the new regulation a powerful instrument. many of your proposals will contribute to our main objective of increasing transparency, particularly on the crucial issue of classification. for example, and this was also mentioned by the rapporteur, in addition to online intermediation services, search engines will have to provide information on any differentiated treatment they apply. companies will now be able to resolve disputes more easily. for example, all platforms, except the smallest, will have to have an internal complaint handling system and use mediators that platforms must identify in advance to allow for out-of-court settlement of disputes. this balance takes account of the expectations of enterprises. the final text also strengthened the role of the member states in implementing the regulation. member states will have to put in place measures to ensure that the new rules are applied properly and effectively. they will also have to work with us, the commission, to monitor and assess the impact of the new rules and share relevant information. but, of course, our work does not end here. we will develop guidelines that will provide more clarity on the information that online platforms and search engines must provide on how ranking works. we will of course work with industry to develop codes of conduct to complement the new rules. i am glad that the compromise we have reached preserves the spirit, the objectives and the approach of our original proposal. it creates a balance and allows platforms, particularly european ones, to establish themselves and develop. this is only the first step. we are already taking the lead, on the one hand with the work of the observatory on the platform economy, and i would remind you that our independent experts are preparing a preliminary report expected later this year, on the other hand with the revision as provided for in the regulation, there will be a rigorous evaluation 18 months after the entry into force. we, for our part, count on your support to continue this process. i also hope that parliament will approve the regulation when it votes tomorrow.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, the key to any business is honesty and transparency with your consumers, but also with your business partners. this regulation ensures this needed greater transparency, while not imposing unneeded over—regulation on platforms and traders. online commerce is the future of our economy and we must not destroy our european players in a misguided attempt to attack the international ones. the path to hell is paved with good intentions. some in this house saw this regulation as a political tool, as a way to change competition policy through the back door. i am pleased that in the end common sense has prevailed and i can only thank the rapporteur in this regard. she had the courage to seek a good and balanced agreement over fighting for one which would have had a negative effect on our economy and the digital single market. this is why the alde group will support the trilogue outcome tomorrow at the votes, and i encourage both platforms and traders to use this new transparency to its greatest extent. it is only by adopting best practices and effective self-regulation that this market can continue to be fair to all. i fear if platforms don’t use this chance that they will not get another one.
mr president, the reason for the negotiated agreement is that it is a change that improves the position of consumers and that if we do not approve it tomorrow, consumers will have to wait another year. this is another example of a double standard, where the rights of some consumers are considered more important than the rights of others, such as those who rightly feel that they are second-class citizens. that's why i voted no for a deal in committee and i've tabled an amendment that, if passed, would put us back on the table. let us not leave it until we have in our hands a text with which we can in good conscience stand before our citizens and tell them that we have indeed outlawed dual-membership. i suggested how to do it. double quality must be a foul practice. only the same products should be labelled, and the consumer should be able to see what he is buying at a glance. no more business favors! ladies and gentlemen, please do not change your opinion under pressure, which we have already expressed, to prohibit double quality. the credibility of the european institutions, of the european union as a whole, is at stake, and i think that is not a small thing.
mr president, at our last meeting we could and should have put an end to the debate on the equal amount of cocoa in chocolate. that was his clear message. for me, as the member of parliament elected for the czech republic, the result is a disappointment. i don't want to be here today looking for a culprit, i don't want to say which lobby won. it could be the pressure of some states, it could be the pressure of those who are filling the shelves with inferior goods. but we can tell exactly who the loser is. he's a consumer, a customer from eastern europe. on the table today is material that has tempered the harsh and uncompromising treatment of our eastern european suppliers, now i apologize for the term, shunts. it's good that after many years of debate, we have new, concrete legislation on the table that is trying to do that. but the list of possible exemptions for manufacturers makes it a soft, symbolic piece of legislation, rather than a law that could actually protect us from unfair business practices. we are giving manufacturers the opportunity to defend, once again, different ingredients and therefore different quality of products. including, for example, excuses for different taste preferences of customers. that is why i am asking my colleagues to support our amendments tomorrow and thus put an end to the practice of having two types of consumers in europe. mr president, a year ago everyone here, starting with the commission, promised us that there would be no more double standards on the eu market. the resulting text, however, shows the opposite and maintains the present reprehensible state of affairs. moreover, under pressure from the commission, which did not directly request this, the minimum requirement to indicate the different composition was dropped from the text. i stress that it was after the final trilogy, in an opaque way, behind the scenes. i must therefore warn you that if the directive as presented is adopted, it will be perceived very negatively by the citizens of the czech republic. on the one hand, the eu is imposing a series of controversial obligations on us, but on the other hand, the western countries are making us into a completely uncovered garbage dump. the risk is that the countries of central and eastern europe will have no choice but to adopt their own national legislation, even at the cost of conflict with eu law. therefore, please support our amendments which restore the original negotiated compromise to the text and combat this shameful practice of double quality. if the results are delayed by a few months, it doesn't matter at this point. because we're going to tell all the citizens of the european union that they're equal. and that's what the amendment is about.
mr president, i am convinced that this directive as a whole is a benefit for consumers. it will help to protect them from the unfair practices they face in all member states, including aggressive door-to-door sales, fake paid reviews on the internet, hidden advertising when searching for goods, deceptive discounts or fraudulent sales of tickets for cultural and sporting events. the most important part, i think, is the explicit inclusion of dual quality as unfair business practices. i am proud that this success is largely due to the czech footprint. i thank mrs sehnall for having drawn attention to this problem for a long time and for having had these activities here in the european parliament, i thank mr jureček, the former agriculture minister, who has promoted this issue in the council, and i thank the commissioner who has prepared this proposal and presented it to the european commission. this adjustment is not perfect. i agree with my colleagues that we should move on to a more precise definition, but we have room here after two years of checking, of revision, after the implementation of this directive, to go back to it and those parts that will not work will be able to be revised. this is explicitly stated in this directive. i think it's a step in the right direction. i will support the amendments, but if they are not adopted, i will support the directive as a whole, because it is important that these practices finally begin to be combated.
mr. chairman, which of us would prefer an ice cream that's made from a substitute but not from cream when it's served under the same wrapper? these are real examples of what we see in the single market in europe. or a round drink that has the lowest form of sweetener instead of the other one that's marketed as being of excellent quality. i think we have to combat these scams, and i think we are going in a very good direction thanks to mrs sehnall and others. equal rights, that can't just be an empty concept. there can be no double standards in europe. it is against the rules of the internal market. we have no europeans of double standards, double quality or second class! i think it's good that we've gotten to this stage where we're supposed to decide on amendments. i signed them and i support them. i regret that the czech government has unfortunately supported the compromise which, in my view, loosens those definitions and is undoubtedly a deterioration of consumer rights, and it is important to go back to those amendments. i'm asking for their support.
madam president, in the previous paragraph we talked about dual quality and now we are also talking about quality, and that is food safety. i think that's no less important than the double standard. this directive addresses an authorisation regime which should be based on clear scientific conclusions. the new regulation should be a good compromise that will increase the transparency of risk assessments carried out by the european food safety authority. at the same time, it should not jeopardise the competitiveness of european companies. greater transparency of scientific studies on food is necessary to prevent misinformation or even conspiracy theories while maintaining a really high level of food safety. on the other hand, companies also have a need for trade secrets that must be respected. therefore, a better result could also be achieved by placing efsa under greater political oversight, including by the european parliament.
madam president, this proposal is a response to the european citizens' initiative against glyphosate, which has very broad public support, and to the monsanto papers case, which reveal the unhealthy environment of producing scientific studies for companies whose products are to be decided on. the commission proposed to enhance the transparency of the risk assessment process and to provide sufficient assurance as to the reliability, objectivity and independence of the studies used by efsa in its risk assessment. i have to say, and you won't hear me say it often here, that the final text is a significant improvement on the current situation, because risk assessment and management is supposed to be more transparent and accountable. the studies should finally be published without hindrance and made available to the general public and to the scientific community, including applications from manufacturers and other supporting documentation. the regulation even contains certain penalties for manufacturers in case of non-transparent behaviour. this is a very important first step in regaining public trust, and i'm happy to support it.
in writing. we have achieved a great result that will increase food safety. we have promoted timely public access to the information of the european food safety authority. this increases the control of the authorization process for substances that we unconsciously ingest through our food. whether it's pesticides, gmos, dyes, flavorings, or other additives, the so-called "e". the safety assessment will be based on the latest scientific knowledge. consultation with third parties shall ensure that no information and no data is hidden. we are introducing more frequent inspections of research institutions and mandatory registration of safety studies. no more hiding inconvenient studies and buying favorable results. this has been proven in some cases. the glyphosate herbicide court case confirmed that. in addition, i was able to get the opening statements to inform the public authorities of cases where our health is endangered by the deliberate deception of manufacturers. it's not fair that we pay for food honestly and get high. and it's not fair at all to gamble our lives for the profits of a few corporations. i am glad that the negotiations have shown that we social democrats are on the side of public health, not on the side of economic interests. it is now up to the member states to increase transparency at their own level. to finally learn how they decide our health.
for the group gue/ngl madam president, eu-based manufacturers of generics and biosimilars are currently not allowed to manufacture products protected by the sps for export to third countries, even if the protection of the products has already expired. it's clear that it's about big money, it's about jobs, but it's also about access to medicines and innovation. we have tried to find a balance between all these complex elements in the envi committee. as expected, however, the outcome of the interinstitutional negotiations is mixed. the biggest problem i see with the text's outcome is the date of its entry into force, or effectiveness. in order for citizens and healthcare systems to benefit from this new regulation, its entry into force must be as soon as possible. the three-year transitional period seems to be a frontier period in this respect. however, from an overall perspective, there should be a gradual improvement in the position of our generic manufacturers, which should benefit everyone. that's why i'm able to support the outcome.
madam president, we voted today for a directive which states, among other things, that 'whistleblowers play a key role in detecting and preventing breaches of the law that harm the public interest and in ensuring the well-being of society as a whole'. that's what we voted for today. in this context, i consider it necessary to emphasise that all the major scandals that have been revealed by the public since 2014, such as luxleaks or the panama papers, have been exposed by whistleblowers and have been clearly in the public interest. i honestly don't care if you consider julian assange a hero or a deserter, a traitor or a freedom fighter. i consider that secondary in the current situation. the only thing i think is really crucial is that if we want to protect whistleblowers, we have to protect assange. he is to be extradited to a country whose crimes he has exposed and whose president is now someone who considers journalists enemies of the people. i don't think he's getting a fair trial there.

member of the commission. – mr president, this is an important file showing our intention first to maintain, but also to strengthen, the eu’s position as a second space power. in line with the results of the negotiation, the commission would like to make five statements on configurations of the committee, member states’ participation, sst and related implementing acts, access to space, security configuration of the committee and copernicus market development as a core task of the eu agency. this text will be given to the services of the european parliament. commission statements(in writing) configurations of the committee: “as regards the introduced by the co-legislators configurations of the committee (within the meaning of regulation (eu) n0 182/2011), the commission notes that the member states remain free and solely responsible to choose their representatives in the committee and the commission does not interpret this provision as conferring on it any power to indicate or select the right experts for a particular configuration.” member states’ participation in sst and related implementing act: “in the understanding of the commission, its empowerment under article 56(4) of the regulation includes inter alia, as indicated in recital 65a, establishing whether offers submitted by member states under article 56(3) should relate to all or some orbits. the commission furthermore understands that article 56(3) allows the presentation of several joint proposals, each of them by at least 5 member states and that the commission may select several offers to maximise the participation of member states in sst.” access to space: “as stated in recital 4, in view of the limited budget, activities under article 5 on access to space should be without prejudice to the main implementation of the programme components indicated in article 3 (a) to (e). the commission therefore intends to prioritise the implementation of the programme’s components.” security configuration committee for security:“the commission emphasises that, due to the sensitivity of the security aspects of the programme and in accordance with article 3(4) and article 6(2) of regulation (eu) 182/2011, it is particularly important that the chair of the programme committee meeting in the security configuration makes every effort to find solutions which command the widest possible support on the security aspects of the programme within the committee.” copernicus market development as core task of the eu agency:“the commission regrets that additional tasks (copernicus market development) will be given to the eu agency while it would not have the resources to cope with them. bearing in mind the overall resources constraints, the agency may have to cope with these new activities by means of internal redeployment, notably through seeking further synergies and efficiencies.”

for the last five years, despite being accused of wrongdoing. endocrine disruptors are everywhere, in food and water, in their packaging, in our cosmetics, in our medicines, and even in the toys we give our children. there is no scientific doubt about the many harmful effects these substances have. we've given manufacturers enough time to come up with some solutions themselves. they didn't come up with anything, of course. on the contrary, we've discovered how they like to manipulate facts and scientific studies. clearly, conflicts of interest among scientists need to be addressed more intensively than we have done so far. endocrine disruptors must be strictly regulated as must carcinogens and mutagens or substances toxic to reproduction. that means we need regulation in all the sectors where they are currently present. we need to make sure that we phase them out as soon as possible. to do this, we need to set mandatory requirements for their testing and identification. it is the next item is the debate on the report by deirdre clune, on behalf of the committee on transport and tourism, on the proposal for a regulation of the european parliament and of the council establishing a european maritime single window environment and repealing directive 2010/65/eu (com(2018)0278 – c8-0193/2018 – 2018/0139(cod)) (a8-0006/2019).
member of the commission. – mr president, the commission is very happy that the legislators have reached a political agreement on this regulation establishing a european maritime single window environment. we will finally now be able to respond to the long-standing request of the eu maritime industry for a fully-harmonised environment for ship reporting in all european ports. i would particularly like to thank the rapporteur, ms clune, as well as the committee on transport and tourism (tran) shadow rapporteurs – it’s great to see you all here today – for their work and determined efforts that allowed an agreement to be reached in a smooth and quick way. today, shipping operators spend around one to three hours per port calling for reporting. the new rules will cut the reporting time by 60%, saving about 2.5 million hours every year, and will hence reduce administrative costs by up to eur 725 million for the shipping sector by 2030. this will be made possible by the adoption of a common set of data definitions and standards; the integration of the existing national maritime single windows in a common interoperable horizontal environment; and the third: the provision of a harmonised interface for such reporting. the new environment will also facilitate sharing and reuse of data and implementation of the ‘reporting once’ principle. when fully implemented, the environment will ensure that a ship reports in the same way, no matter to which port in the eu she will be calling at. in addition, the entire logistics network will benefit from a more efficient flow of information. the shipping sector considers this regulation one of the most relevant initiatives in terms of the improvement of operational efficiency and reduction of administrative burdens, and it has already expressed great satisfaction with the compromise text. i believe that the compromise text not only satisfies all the requests of the three institutions and of the maritime sector but will also benefit the entire logistics network by promoting digitalisation and a more efficient flow of information, which, of course, has a direct impact on competitiveness and the possibility for the maritime sector to be fully integrated in multi-modal solutions.
mr van de camp, i think that if the house had been full now, which it unfortunately, is not, you would get significant applause as one of the key figures, not just in the committee on transport and tourism (tran). and if i may just reciprocate, it was a real pleasure to work with you among the coordinators. i am sure this house will miss you, and thanks very much indeed.

restress how important i believe the adoption of this regulation is for a truly european maritime single window and maritime single area. the environment will profit, our organisation will profit, we will increase efficiency, and of course shipping will become even more competitive. just one additional clarification regarding the port community systems that mr van de camp raised: the commission will not rule out other possible reporting channels, provided that the interfaces for the single maritime window are available and operators are offered the choice. this will be a choice for the member states. on the other hand, port community systems will be able to use data services and provide additional value added services in ports for maritime operators, of course if they comply with the author. mr president, we have before us a motion for a resolution which draws attention to the plight of religious and ethnic minorities in china. it's not the first time. again and again, we are returning to the unacceptable situation that exists in china, the most populous country in the world, a country with a rich spiritual tradition. i'm very pleased that we found such a broad consensus, and i want to thank all the co-authors for being able to find a real consensus despite the different approaches. it's very important because china is important to us. it's important that china uphold its own constitution, the right to freedom of religion. so far, it's the other way around. christians are being persecuted, churches are being destroyed, clergy are being arrested. in my own country, the czech republic, we have over a hundred asylum seekers from china. this resolution draws attention to the persecution of ethnic minorities such as tibetans and uyghurs. the latter are also victims of the recently reestablished concentration camp system and electronic surveillance. in this context, we are mentioning specific cases of people and calling for their immediate release. for all of them, i will mention, for example, the life-sentence of the uyghur economist, intellectual, defender of the rights of the people, ilham tohti. will we ever see him released? i hope so, just as we waited for the release of nelson mandela or václav havel. we call for sanctions against those involved in the ongoing repression in xinjiang province. that's why i'm also agreeing to send an independent mission to this province. the chances of the chinese regime restoring human rights on its own are slim. that is why the european union must stand firm.
mr. chairman, today i purposely wore a red tie, the color of blood, the color of the suffering of the people persecuted by communist china. it's not just tibet, but it's mainly christians. churches are being demolished, bibles are being confiscated, clergymen are being imprisoned. an estimated 50 million, as many as half of china's christians are persecuted. clergy have to report to police stations as if they were criminals. people under the age of 18 are prohibited from attending services. in these circumstances, the european union must make a clear statement at every high-level meeting condemning the discrimination against christians in china. the european union and its member states must also draw the consequences of this persecution when chinese citizens seek asylum in europe. otherwise we are not a europe based on the protection of fundamental rights.

group id. mr president, the grant of a patent for a tomato variety and the anti-cancer property of a broccoli variety by the european patent office is in breach of the first un convention on biological diversity, the second nagoya protocol to that convention, the third european patent convention and its implementing protocol. all member states are parties to the european patent convention. each of our countries has its representative on the administrative council of the european patent organisation, which has the power to settle such disputes over patents issued by the european patent office. we therefore propose that the member states, through their representatives on the administrative council of the european patent office, should force the office and its officials to, first, annul the patents already granted on varieties and their properties and, second, ensure that nothing like this ever happens again, either in the 240 cases examined or elsewhere.

madam president, the scandals that have been exposed in recent years – and we’ve heard about them, we’ve seen millions and in some cases billions of euros being laundered – have left the people we represent fed up. they are fed up with always being the ones to bear the burden of the consequences when there are economic downturns, and fed up that the richest in our society and criminals are able to find ways to stash their cash away from regulatory oversight and tax collection. just one example: as we heard earlier, danske bank facilitated eur 200 billion of suspicious transactions over eight years in estonia. despite executives facing criminal prosecution in several countries, the european banking authority this year rejected proposals that there had been breaches of union law. are they serious? the commission is still prevaricating. a few weeks ago it said it was still examining the matter. there are many, many more examples. we cannot simply wait for another big scandal. there are too many loopholes in our legislation that give opportunities to criminals and those who want to bend the rules. if we don’t fight this kind of cross-border criminality here, where will it be done? how is the commission going to ensure we get tougher on this issue? we have to go further with the tools available to us, and the legislation has to be implemented with vigour in all 28 member states. just one quick question to the commission: what are we doing to investigate the thousands of shell companies registered in offshore tax havens, who else is linked to them and what are their purposes?
madam chairman, preventing money laundering is a key issue in the fight against organized crime. but it is also a matter of maintaining public confidence and enforcing the principle of transparency. i appreciate the shift that the fourth and fifth anti-money laundering directives bring. the problem is that many countries have not even implemented the fourth directive sufficiently. so what's the next step? citizens trust the european commission and rely on its ability to enforce the law. it is impossible to discredit their trust, commissioner jourová. public registers of the ultimate owners of companies are a fundamental shift. but if we want effective public scrutiny, we need to be able to work with this data effectively, it needs to be machine-readable. as we have seen in the czech republic, trust funds need to be transparent. the registry of the real owners of the trusts is a good step forward so far. but the commission must already ensure that member states do not restrict access to these registers to journalists or non-profit organisations, for example. we shouldn't repeat the mistakes that happened in the past.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, with this resolution we call on the member states to implement effectively the european legislation adopted in the field of combating money laundering in the european union, namely the fourth and fifth anti-money laundering directives. our criticism is justified, given that a large number of member states have not yet transposed the rules of the fourth directive correctly and have to introduce the provisions of the fifth directive within a few months. this is particularly important given that the objective of disrupting the sources of terrorist financing is to prevent attacks on the territory of the european union. it is a fact that the current european union framework on anti-money laundering is suffering from implementation shortcomings. it is therefore important to build a comprehensive european approach to combat money laundering and terrorist financing as effectively as possible. the european union needs to be able to address money laundering and combat terrorist financing in a way that is effective and autonomous, in particular as regards the inclusion of third countries on the eu blacklist.
madam president, it is clear that tackling money laundering is a major policy issue and cross—border challenge. fragmentation in national implementation and supervisory practices in relation to anti—money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism is aggravating the problems we face. we must find a way for better enforcement of eu rules. in the framework of the fifth anti-money laundering (aml) directive, a number of corrective measures have been taken to address the existing shortcomings. it is important that, in transposing the directive into national legislation, member states work together on critical points which have been identified, including poor information sharing at both supervisory level and the level of financial institutions themselves. information sharing is key and we should make better use of new technologies in this regard, with the aim of improving the interconnectivity of relevant databases so as to increase access to data and facilitate cross—border cooperation. also, as new challenges arise, such as broader use of crypto-assets, we need to adapt our approach to make sure these are covered by our aml framework. time is of the essence, so we all need to take responsibility and act together swiftly to stem the flow of illegal money in europe.
the debate is closed. the vote will be held at the end of the debates. written statements (rule 171)

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, today we commemorate the anniversary of the velvet revolution, a non-violent positive change of regime in czechoslovakia. thanks to our previous generation, we live in a free society and we managed to integrate well into the european structures. however, the current generation needs to address another difficult task – to cope with the misuses of the tools the digital era brings and to ensure that our state works for everyone equally and is not providing benefits only to a few. twice this year, more than 250 000 people gathered in letná park in prague to express their willingness to defend our democracy and liberties in the biggest demonstrations in the czech republic since 1989. a memento of the velvet revolution is protection of freedom. i strongly believe this cannot be silenced and that in the end, as václav havel said: ‘truth and love will overcome lies and hatred’.
madam president, today marks exactly 30 years since 800,000 people gathered in prague to demand an end to one-party rule. behind this miracle, which i call the miracle of the revolution, there were thousands of victims, but also the hard work and the many sacrifices that had to be made. over the years, the countries behind the iron curtain have had to overcome many obstacles, including their success in joining the european union. this anniversary should lead us to understand what unites us, or why new barriers are being created and how to confront them, whether it is called social dumping or protectionism. we in central europe should not forget the value of freedom, democracy and the rule of law. we should be saying, "thank you for letting us do this. thank you for the freedom.

madam chair, 30 years after the fall of communism, it's easy to forget how violent that system was and how it was opposed to everything we consider the achievements of civilization. in post-communist countries, nostalgic voices, interestingly, only mention industrialization and some kind of security, but they skillfully avoid talking about systematic human rights violations and regime violence that have crossed national borders. croatian political emigrants were thus liquidated by yugoslav intelligence services all over the world. the regime's opponents were nowhere safe, not even as full citizens of democratic, western states. it's important to talk about this today so that the new generations know all the evils of communism and socialism. it is not a system of social justice and equality, but a criminal regime that has made human life a mere statistic and has killed millions.
madam president, i was almost ten years old at the time of the events of november 1989. we lived near the place where the special forces were coming out. i still remember the two emotions in my parents' eyes and in their voices. it was fear and it was hope. hope has won. we had free elections, freedom of speech, we split up with our slovak colleagues, but only to find ourselves in the european union and nato again. with the fall of the iron curtain and the end of the cold war, history did not end, even though we may have thought it did for a while. freedom is not a given today, the fight for human rights is not a fight of the past. we should not forget that. what does that mean for us? we should not forget all the sacrifices that have been made over the decades to be able to be here today, part of a free and democratic europe, and to fight for these ideals into the future. we should also avoid making wrong decisions. when we look around us, at crimea, or when we look at the recent decisions on the balkans, we have to remember these facts.
madam president, we are now in a situation where the future budget of the european union will be co-decided by the czech prime minister, andrej babiš, whose vital interest is subsidies for his own companies. the financial plans for europe for the next seven years are being decided by someone who is motivated by his own personal gain, someone who has, for example, like a lion, opposed the accumulation of agricultural subsidies because it is beneficial for his large agricultural enterprises. please, let's take this situation seriously. i would ask the council to take the conflict of interest in this context into account and exclude andrey babiš from the budget negotiations. otherwise, we will lose both money and the trust of our citizens, because we will not be able to make decisions based on the basic principles of just governance. the board is already facing a lawsuit over this, and i don't think there's any point in dragging this out any longer.
mr president, 30 years after the ratification of the un convention on the rights of the child, many efforts have yielded results, yet much more still needs to be done to fully protect children. the european pirates want the public authorities and police forces to have the human capacity to properly respond to the threat of criminals sharing child abuse material over the internet. we should insist on human intervention via internet hotlines, enabling the exchange of information, and helplines assisting victims of abuse. calling on private companies to monitor the entire internet in order to make sure that nobody is sending wrong images is, however, not the way forward. when effective law enforcement is increasingly outsourced to private companies and their dominant technologies with very little public intervention, we are only helping to hide the representation of abuse without any guarantee of investigation and prosecution of criminals. while children’s rights need to be protected for the reasons mentioned, we unfortunately could not support the motion for a resolution.
mr. chairman, i would like to follow up on my speakers from the pirate delegation, marcel kolaj and patrick breyer. i believe that the protection of children's rights should not be taken lightly and i want to protect them. but i do not agree with the procedure that has been inadvertently inserted into this text in the recital and article 16. that is why i abstained and i stand by my decision.
